WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. NATIONAL REGISTRATION ACT, 1915.

NATIONAL REGISTRATION. IMPORTANT NOTICE. • DUTY TO REGISTER. 1. Every person between the ages, of 15 and -65 (with certain exceptions) is required to fill UP, and sign a form of registration stating name; Place of residence; .age; and certain other par- ticulars.

A form of registration' to be filled up' and signed will be left at the place of residence of each person before Stinday, the rfith of August, 1915.

PLACES WHERE NEW FORMS MAY BE

OBTAINED

2. Any person between the ages of 15 and 65 who may not before Sunday, the 15th of August, 1915, have received a form of registration, or who may have lost, or destroyed, or spoilt it, or who has otherwise been prevented from filling Up the form on or before Sunday, the 15th of August, 1915, may obtain a form by applying at ANY POST OFFICE WITHIN THE WAR- RINGTON RURAL DISTRICT, on the 16th, 17th, and 18th of August. 1915. Also at the fol- lowing Post Offices: Folly-lane, Padgate-lane, Sankey Bridges.

ATTENDANCE BEFORE LOCAL REGISTRA-

TION AUTHORITY.

3. If a person liable to fill up and sign a form of. registration has not done so, or if the form which he or she has filled up, signed and returned, is incomplete or incorrect, the Local Registration Authority may require such per- Oil to attend before them to answer such ques- tions as may be necessary' to enable such form -to be filled up properly.

CHANGE OF ADDRESS. 4. If any 'person registered • under the above Act changes his or her place of residence and has • come to reside within this area, he or she shall, unless such change is merely temporary, within twenty-eight days thereafter send or deliVer to the Local Registration Authority of this area at the address given below by post or Otherwise, his or her certificate of registration With the new place of residence noted thereon. Such person shall thereupon be entitled to be supplied with a fresh certificate of registration. • NEW ARRIVALS 114 THE UNITED KINGDOM TO REGISTER. 5: Within twenty-eight days after the arrival in the United Kingdom of any person between the ages of 15 and 65 he or she shall, if et previously registered, and if residing in area, send or deliver to the Local Registra- tion Authority of ,this area by post or other- "se, notice of his or her arrival, together with Particulars concerning himself or herself re- Tiired by the above-mentioned Act to be regis- tered. He or she shall, if so required, attend ftt such place and time as, the Authority may. LlPPoint and shall answer such questions as may _B addressed to him- or her for the purpose of Znabling the necessary particulars to he regis- :red, and thereupon shall be supplied with a, jrtificate of registration. A form of registra- ,:loll to be filled up and signed may be obtained. 03r such person from the undersigned.

PENALTItS. G• Refusal or neglect to observe the above PYovisions is, in the case of a person over "_elghteen years of age required to register him- "ova or herself, an offence for which the party end,ing is liable on conviction to a fine not ~ ..e'eeuing £5 and in the case of a continuing eactiue to a further fine not exceeding £1 for (5 " daY during whicl4 the offence continues and 6, Geo. 5, C. GO, s. 13 (3)). J. C. SUTTON, Clerk.. aelvseY-Chambers. • Warrington, 3rd August, 1915.

FARM AND GARDEN.

.

P. THE BOY ON THE FARM. 1 lla told his 12-year-old son to milk the cows, feed the horses, slop the pigs, hunt up the eggs, 1 feed the calves, catch the colt and 0,4. the stable, cut some wood, split at) kindling, stir the cream, pump fresh water into the creamery after supper, and be sure to study his lesson before he went to bed. Then he went to the Farratirs) Club to discuss the question "How to'ketp the boys on the farm."

HARVESTING PREPARATIONS.

I do not remember an instance in which a first-rate 'farmer has not made a point of mak- ing all possible preparations for successfully and quickly harvesting his crops long before har- vest commences. The man who is to drive the binder is required to overhaul it, to clean it, and to put it in thorough condition, so far as his part of the work is concerned. Binder-string is obtained, some buyers making their selections at the Royal Show, although this sale is quite late enough.. The machine for sharpening the knives is got ready for work, a new knife with plenty of sections, if these are required, is ordered from the maker of the binder, and in the old days the sheets were repaired 'on the fa-rm. The whole equipment, however, is of necessity examined. Some farmers make a practice of threshing in the field; where this plan is decided on it is wise of a flockmaster to determine where he intends to erect his lambing fold, so that straw ricks may be built both for the sake of the straw—thus saving haulage at a busy time— and of their presence as wind breaks—than which nothing is of much greater importance in an exposed situation. Straw ricks are thatched on a well-managed farm, and this year in par: Ilcular the practice should not be omitted, as -straw is a food of great value, and may be re- quired" to the- last truss for feeding the stock. Thatching, therefore involves the preparation .of the yealms, the; provision of stakes or spars, and of twine, where this is used instead of twist- ing the straw into ropes. So far oapossible, too, the ricks should be built near to water, which is so highly essential-in thatching.—Jas. Long in "Farm and Home." -

===== 0002601_19150807_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
LABOUR ON THE FARM.

Although there are complaints in some quar- ters, farmers, as a rule, seem satisfied with the condition of field work; and no acute difficulty seems probable at present. A good - number, of farm lads joined the army in May, but sufficient appear to have remained. In some cases younger boys than usually go out have been attracted by the high, wages offered. Many farmers have also reduced their staff, and waggoners may be met with who are feeding five horses each instead of the former maximum of four. One farmer working ten horses had previously three wag- goners. He now has two, who do five horses each. The wages given at May-day averaged a general advance of X. 6.—" Agricultural Econo- mist and Horticultural Review."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
SWEATING IN THE STACK.

Thatching a hay-stack while sweating, says Warwickshire in "Farm and Home," does not 'materially affect the hay in the way of caus- ing mould, save near the outside of roof; there the confinement of the steam by the covering causes mould. But a worse thing than this has to be considered in thatching a stack before sweating has ceased, • and that is the settling. While active heating goes on, the stack con- tinues to settle, and very often in peculiar or erratic ways. You cannot put the riek in ship- shape after it has been thatched, so that it may, through settling down all awry, take a good deal of damage when a rainy or what is worse, a snowy season sets in. Nor is that all. The roof contracting through the consolidation brought about by sweating, and the thatch not shrink- ing with the hay, the straw covering bends be- tween the bands almost to bow shape, and does precious little good by way of keeping out rain or snow. Thus, to thatch a hay or clover stack while sweating is going on actively is foolish in the extreme, and does more harm than if the rick were not covered' at all.

. _ Therefore, one should take the settling of the stack as a guide in deciding when to thatch. Settling may scarcely go on at all, or it may go on for several days or several weeks; all de- pends upon the condition of the hay when carted in.. But whether the settling goes on for a long or short time, it is caused by sweating, and it will be obvious enough when the material -ceases to shrink, and that is the time to pull, trim and thatch.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
MAKING THE PIG WORK.

What is known as a hog motor is said to be on exhibition at the Panama Pacific Exposition. The plan is to make the hogs grind their own feed. The whole grain, barley and corn, is put in at the top of the mill, which revolves slowly, there being grinders at the bottom of it. The hogs reach in, and with their rooting and push- ing their snouts after the grain they slowly turn this contrivance around, thus crushing or grind- _ _

ing the whole grain. Evidently this grinder is so arranged that it can only be turned in one direction, and the hog being an animal of in- Cfelligence soon finds which way it turns.—" Rural Nair Yorker."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

CABBAGES FOR WINTER AMY SPRING.

Most gardeners have a fixed date for sowing cabbages to stand the winter. When the seed is sown earlier than this fixed date it is very prob- able that the plants will bolt, while if sowing is deferred the chances are that the plants will not have attained a good size before the winter sets in, the crop being correspondingly later. The ground for the seeds should be in good heart, and it is always advisable to sow in drills, which, if the weather is dry, should be well watered on the evening prilvious to sowing the seeds the following morning. In districts where birds are numerous the seed-bed should be pro- tected with nets. To ward off the birds we have found it a good plan to roll the seeds in red lead, care being taken not to damp the seeds too much, as if this is done they are very liable to stick together. Slugs, too, often cause much loss by destroying the young seedlings, but fre- quent dressings of lime and soot will go a long way in checking their attack.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
TUFTED PANSIES (VIOLAS).

These, in spite of the dry weather, have been very good this season, and are still flowering freely, thanks in great measure to the recent heavy rains we have had. If, however, one wishes to see a continuation of bloom, all the faded flowers must be removed and a top dress- ing of manure given, with, if the weather is dry, frequent soakings of water. From plants put out in April, 1914, we have had a continuous .supply of bloom up to the present, while the plants put out in the spring of this year are now coming into bloom, and will continue the 'display into the autumn. Through the kindness of a friend we have procured a few pieces of that old favourite tufted pansy, Violetta, one of the daintiest forms we know. The pure white flowers, though very small, are freely produced, another asset being their sweet perfume.

Farm 1144 Home."

The President of the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries has the authority of the Secretary of State for War to announce that, having regard to the valuable work for the nation which is being done by the engine drivers and mechanics employed by the proprietors of steam ploughs and threshing machinery, it has been decided to extend the concession made in respect of the recruiting of skilled farm hands to these men, who, therefore, should not be induced to enlist. In the event of a difference of opinion arising between recruiting officers and farmers or pro- prietors of agricultural machinery with regard to the enlistment of special men, arrangements have been made for the chairman of each Petty Sessional Division in England and Wales to select a magistrate to act as referee in cases of the kind. Particulars of the case for reference to a magistrate must be stated on a form, which will be supplied by recruiting officers on request. The form, when completed, should be for- warded to the Clerk to the Petty Sessional Divi- sion concerned, who will send it to the selected magistrate and arrange a day convenient to the parties for a hearing. At the hearing only the recruiting officer and the employer will be per- mitted to attend and be heard.

HOME-GROWN SUGAR.

British Farmers' Opportunity To Cripple Germany.

It was the last great European war that saw the sugar-beet industry established on the Continent. Will the 'resent European war see. it established in England? Its beginnings in Europe were due to Eng- land's possession of the cane sugar—then the onlY. sugar—industily ; and to Napoleon's embargo on the import of the West Indian product into France and the other countries I under his domination. Napoleon devoted nearly a quarter of a million acres in France to beet-growing, and made the growers a grant of a million francs. By 1812 he had established no fewer than 40

factories. By thirs rendering Europe independent of British sugar, Napoleon struck a shrewd blow at Britain. British farmers are now discuss- ing steps, which, if taken, will strike a shewder blow at Germany and Austria-Hungary— whence came, until the war, over three-fourths of our beet-sugar supply. "There is no reason why we should not grow all the: sugar we ifeed," says Dr. Augustus Voelcker, the consulting chemist to the Royal Agricultural Society. "The beet sugar industry was successful on the Continent because it was scientifically conducted. The sugar-beet can be grown here as well as, and even better than; in any other country. Our Advantages.

It means hard work and scientific manage- ment, but it is well worth doing. And now, if at all, is the time to do it." The fcillowing list shows the very considerable advantages a British beet-growing industry would have over those on the Continent :- 1. We eall grow. more beets per acre. , 2. We can grow richer roots than those- on ' the 'Continent. 3. Our market 'is at home, since we are the greatest sugar-consumers in the world. 4. We save on freights. 5. We oan 'refine at a lower cost. 6. ' Coal, coke and limestone are cheaper and more available than on the Continent. Important points -in connection with the pro- posed industry are that the closest co-operation should be secured between farmers and manu-' facturers, and that the sugar factories should be established as near as possible to the beet- growing farmers. It is suggested that the Government should encourage industry by the appointment of a Commission of -Control and by making grants. Any skilled labour re- quired, as "The Daily Chronicle" pointed out in September last, could be supplied by refugees from the beet-growing districts of Belgium and France.

RIVAL CLAIMS OF WAR AND THE

LAND.

Farm Hands Who Should Not Be

Induced To Enlist.

FARM AND GARDEN.—Continued.

WALLFLOWERS AND CARNATIONS.

Plant out seedling wallflowers six inches apart on land that has had a crop of something on it. No manure is necessary; the plants will with- stand the winter better without. . Now is the time to layer carnations. • This is done by making an upward slit of about half an inch in the stem between two joints._Then press down to the ground, and hold firmly in position by a hairpin, or wire shaped like a hairpin, preferably with one end longer than the other. Next cover over with soil. What cannot be layered should be cut off and slit up half an inch and put in a cold frame, where they will root readily. Keep the slit open so that they will root faster, and shade with paper for a day or two.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKS, ROSES, &c.

Seedling Brompton stocks can be planted out on a south border shaded from cold winds. Sow more if necessary. Also -Canterbury Bells and Sweet Williams may be sown, but it is rather late to get good strong plants. Keep early chrysanthemums well staked up to prevent the wind from breaking them. Stake up gladiolus, and feed plants with weak liquid manure. Asters have had a very bad season this year, but the recent rains have made them "jump" wonderfully. These may be given a dose of weak liquid manure. Cuttings of roses may be inserted in sandy soil in a shady 'position. Such types as Dorothy Perkins and Crimson Rambler will root readily. Put in the cuttings slanting or at an angle of 45 degrees; this causes the bottom buds to throw up good shciots when they are rooted.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
VEGETABLES AND FRAMES.

Vegetables such a 4 cabbage, curly greens or kale, and broccoli should be planted extensively while the war is on. Plants of these may be got in as soon as possible, and a small sowing of cabbage for spring should he sown at once. Ellam'S Early and Flower of Spring are good sorts.

For frames (while glass is dear), those who have them are advised to wait till glass drops in price, covering their frames this winter with netting or any such thing as a protection from birds. When severe weather sets in mats or sacks can be laid on the netting to exclude the frost somewhat. _

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 7, 1915.

WARRINGTON AGRICULTURAL

SOCIETY.

War. Time Show a Success Despite Curtailment.

ENTRIEs FEW, BUT 6000,

The twenty-sixth annual show in connection with the Warrington and District Agricultural Society was held in the Brewery Field, Wilders- pool, on Thursday, and—despite the fact that the schedule of classes had been curtailed seriously on account of the war, and that the customary amusements in the shape of exhibi- tion horse-jumping, driving, &c., to say nothing of the band, had been dispensed, with—was a considerable success,t hough naturally, having regard to all the circumstances, both the entry and the attendance Were smaller than in normal years. To, begin with there were but fourteen clasSes,..corapetition being confined to breeders and -owners of Shires, dairy cattle,

heifers and bulls Despite the limitations imposed by circum- stances, there was an encouraging entry, and although in 'number the animals entered Aid not come within hundreds of 'previous years, the quality was decidedly good, the judges„ without exception, being enthusiastic in praise of the live stock that cameunder their notice. The Brewery Field made an almost ideal venue for a show of the size and, despite the restricted catalogue, there was an encouraging attend- ance of breeders and rearers of cattle and of agriculturists in general, though, as was only to be expected, comparatively few of the general. public." parted. up" at the gates. . The number of entries,- of course, bare no comparison with last year's grand total, but it came fully up to the expectations of the com- mittee; indeed, everything considered, it was regarded as surprisingly goad, the fourteen •- , classes fetching no fewer than 87 entries, and all of them producing some competition, whilst not .a few attracted quite a:respectable number of entries 'and gave the. judges a considerable amount of trouble—that sort of trouble which

YEAR OF WAR.

Comments From The Local

Parish Magazines.

Without exception the editors of the local parish magazines refer in their August issues to the fact that this month sees the end Of the first twelve months of the great•war,...

• The Rector (Rev. Canon Willis), in" The Parish Church Parochial Magazine" says : "The anniversary of the declaration of war— August 4th—should be Marked, we feel, by a new start, as it were, in the efforts we are mak- ing to take our full share in supplying our coun- try's needs. Of course, one of the best things we can do is to pray more freetuently and more fervently. We propose to hold an outdoor ser- vice on this day. We hope all our congregation will give us all the support they can."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
"EVER MEMORABLE IN HISTORY."

The Rev. H. B. Firth, vicar of St. James',, Latchford, writing on the same subject in his magazine, says: " On the fourth of August Eng- land will have been at war with Germany for a year. The date will be ever memorable in the history of our country as. the beginning of the greatest struggle in which we and our Allies have been engaged since the world began. . , . Certainly the next few vieeks will be fraught with consequences of the utmost importance. While none of us ought to be despondent, the situation affords no ground for light-hearted confidence; the fate of the Empire lies in the balance, and every effort that the nation can exert, whether spiritual, material, or physical, is urgently needed." FEWER CASUALTIES AMONG ST. ANN'S

===== 0002601_19150807_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
MEN.

The Vicar of St. Ann's (the Rev. Jas. Davies) 41 so refers to the war in his monthly magazine. Says he :—" As 'a district we have much c:tuse to be thankful. The toll of killed, wounded and missing has not been for us nearly so great during July, and possibly the month as a whole will show a less total. We sincerely trust it may be so. The strife still continues and on even a more extended scale. The enemy are straining all their powers; and may succour .Divine and human come to the aid of our Allies and give nein decisive:victory. We have great reason for thanksgiving also in the success granted to our forces in South West Africa. and the conclusion of strife in that 'vast area."

In another part of 'his letter Mr. Frost talks of those who are afraid of being laughed at. What a miserable excuse! Better be a clown than a coward, especially in an honourable cause. It is easier to take smiles than sneers. You talk about being laughed at! Last winter I was told off to drill a squad of recruits (like myself) and instead of giving them "left wheel" I gave them "right wheel," and turned .them. point blank against a stone wall, to the intense amusement of about 300 or 400 "laughing" onlookers. But 'what did I care? Not a brace-button! I can say to-day, and with, I venture, pardonable pride, that I wear. a marksman's badge on the right arm of my uniform. You. see, Mr.. Editor, I am- Married. I have a wife and two -children: They may need protection and so I am getting ready iu spite of laughter. But they have ceased to laugh at the Doncaster • contingent of the Voluntary Training Corps. We can muster over a hundred for church parade; we are respected by all, including every branch of ' His Majesty's Regular Forces who chance to be stationed in the town. And why not the 'Same of good old Grappenhall? Get to your drills, my lads, and into your uniform. Last week-end we went to camp. at Firbeck Hall Park, about 12 miles from Doncaster. On the Sunday night I was told off for sentry duty. It was a wet, doleful night. • I had hard Work to see the face of the moon. through the mist, and as I wearily marched the "Flying Sentry" around the tents, I almost fell asleep.' Had it not been that an occasional tent-peg tripped me up I believe I should have done so., But there was something more in connection with Firbeck Park that helped to keep me awake. It was 2.30 in the morning; the sky became a little clearer; my vision carried me to the south-west side of this park and I looked Upon that fine old stone mansion (Firbeck Hall) in the distance. The regular occupant is absent on military duties. Other people occupy this stately old mansion now. A cottage home has been destroyed in Belgium; many cottage homes have been destroyed there; divided families ha:ye been taken in the bosom of old England. Part of them are resident in Firbeck Hall. A fatal bayonet wound from the cruel monsters of Prussia have caused these stricken people to leave a portion of their families behind. Little children, remember! And you are afraid of being laughed at when you go to drill in order that you may be ready to meet, if needs he,..theseYrussian Devils. Never mind what your past opinions were, or what thei may be now. Your own flesh and blood is in need of protection. Get a move on, men of Grappenhall, and rally round Mr. Frost. Never mind if you are laughed at! "He who laughs last, laughs best." Every little helps and remember always that ~ou are out to kill the vilest disease that has ever stricken a nation. It is only, by welding together all our resources that we can ever_ hope to bring about the time when, as J. A. Symonds says: Nation with nation, land with land, Inarmed shall live as comrades free; In every heart and brain shall throb The pulse of one fraternity. —Yours, &c.;

GEO. A. LEACH.

R., High-street, Doncaster.

YOU LIKE "THE EXAMINER "?

Of course you do.

But do you get it regularly?

Do you advise your friends to see the good things it contains?

Do you send it to Your soldier and sailor boys, who are longing for the latest and best news from home?

If you go away in the summer for a week or a month do you have it posted to your holiday address? Your newsagent is anxious to have your instructions to send it on to you.

Or we will be glad to post the paper direct from The Examiner" Office if you will send us your name and address and the

tecestary cash.

The charms of the English Lakes are world famous. They are rarely appreciated, how- ever, to the full extent by the cyclist or the motorist, probably owing to the fact that " going " is supposed to be so heavy. And so it is in places, but however hilly the roads or rough the pas.ses one is more than repaid by the beauty of the countryside. One can't have it both waytt. If there are charming valleys and rugged mountains the .roada must be :hilly and in places rough. A start should be Made from Kendal, following the Barrow-road to a mile beyond Newby Bridge, where the turning to the right should be taken from Hawk's Head and. Coniston, beautifully situated on the lake of that name. Thence to Anibleside, Rydal; Grasmere (to reach which a light detour is necessary) and. Wythburn (at the foot of Helvellyn) to Keswick. From Keswick the Buttermore round should be taken, passing • through Grange, Rosthwaite, Scatoller— situated midway between the lovely lake of Buttermore and the stern and forbidden Crum- Mock Water—and over, the wild Honister Pass (the steepest ascent and descent in England). The run back to Keswick Via the NewlandS Vale is' Very fine. From Keswick the Penrith- road is taken, passing en route Threlkeld, Troutbeck and Penruddock. The run to Ambleside, along the side of Ullswater, is one of the grandest in the lake district. Kirk- stone Pass, nearly 1,000 feet in height, is a stiff clinth, and the descent has to be negotiated with care. The "Travellers' Best" at the summit is claimed to be the highest inhabited licensed house in England, but it is only a local claim. There are three higher. The run back to Keswick is via Windermere and Stave- ley. A slight detour can be made—adding only three-quarters of a mile—by turning to the left, when a'quarter of a mile south of Winder- mere, and riding along the west side of the lake. This 'road is Much better and prtittier than the main road. The. entire distance of the trip is only 120 i miles. The tour can be added to by making the run from Keswick around Bassenthwaite lake and by visiting Langdale, Wastwater and Ennerdale, but the route as described above includes all the most attractive spots.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
300,000 VOLUNTEERS.

Ready For" German Mad Dogs' " Raid.

On the Roodee, at Chester, on Monday, there was an imposing parade of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment—to which a Stockton Heath section is attached—for inspection by General Sir O'Moore Creagh, V.C. Heavy showers somewhat spoiled the spectacle but, nevertheless, a large crowd of onlookers was much imprlessed by the parade. The enrolled strength of the regiment is between 8,000 and 9,000, but it was not possible to arrange for the whole regiment to be present. Neverthe- less, there was a field parade state of about 4,300. The remainder will probably be inspected later. Addressing the officers, General Sir O'Moore Creagh mentioned that the Volunteers in this country numbered at least 304,000, and speak- ing of the future, he said: "The longer the war goes on—and I have studied war all my life—the more certain I am that no man is justified in saying there will not be a raid on England until the German fleet is under the sea. ,I am also certain that the German people just now are like so many mad dogs. They, will take a bite wherever they can, and we have to be ready for them. The Volunteer movement, when the men are efficient and trained, certainly will be invaluable to our country. As sure as I stand here you'll all have your job in due course, and the only thing you have to do is to be ready for it when it comes."

STOCKTON HEATH DETACHMENT.

The Stockton Heath detachment who took part

in the review paraded in uniform at 10 a.m., under the command of Platoon-Commander F. Warburton, and were conveyed by motor to Chester.

Despite the fact that for over four hours, whilst,the detachment was on parade at Chester, it rained in torrents, the members were all of the opinion that Bank Holiday had been well spent. Several Warringtonians, who were spec- tators at the review, congratulated the detach- ment upon their steadiness and correct dressing during the march past. The detachment is now over 90 strong. It ought to be double that number, but it is evi- dent, that a good many eligible men in the vil- lage`have not realised ;their responsibilities. It is not too late to join, and recruits will be wel- come at any time.

MR. F. A. FROSTS LAMENT.

TO PREVENT WASTE OF FOOD.

The Market Gardeners', Nurserymen's and Farmers' Association have decided to ask kindred societies to join it in endeavouring to bring home to the, public the importance of home-grown fruit and vegetables as, food in view of the high prices of bread, meat and groceries. During some recent years very large quantities of garden produce have been thrown away at Covent Garden because the supply exceeded the demand, and in the hope of pre- venting such waste during the present summer a committee is to be appointed to make known the best methods of :Making use Of the great supplies ef fruit and vegetables available.

CLEANING THE CHAIN.

A gear case is a valuable acquisition, hut curiously the greater number of cycles are not fitted with them. To ensure the smooth and easy running of a machine with an uncovered chain, it is essential to see that the 6hain is kept thoroughly,clean and weThoiled. If regu- lar attention be not paid to this, in time the chain will work stiff, and necessitate kgreater expenditure of 'energy than is either desirable or necessary. If attended to frequently there is no need to take the chain off the machine. It can be cleaned effectively in the .following irdn- rer : It should be wiped clean with a soft cloth, or some engineer's waste, and then brushed with a hard brush soaked in paraffin oil. This will remove the dirt and dust which may have accumulated in the joints. A good lubricant should then be applied to the chain and to the cogs on the hub and sprocket.

EARLY RISING.

It may be that people are constituted differently, since some revel in early rising and others delight in staying in bed till the last moment. I3eds are certainly magnetic, but the glories- of the country in the early hours have a much greater drawing power for those who have seen the world at its best. Now that the fine weather may be expected to last for a while, cyclists would do well to give early morning riding a trial. If they are real cycling enthusiasts—i.e., able to appreciate not only the pleasure of movement, but Nature at its best— they will continue the practice until the dark mornings return. AVERAGE SPEED.

There appear .to be many ideas as to what the term "average speed" really applies. Solo take it to mean the number of hours frora• start to finish, including stops for meals, &C., divided into the total mileage. Again, others divide the number of hours the engine is run- ning into the distance covered, ignoring alto- gether any stops whatever, whether duo to trouble with the machine or from other causes. If we are to compare the performances Of different motor cycles I think the time spent on the road attending to the machine should be added to the hours the engine is going, be' fore the division is made. One machine maY not be quite so fast on the road, but more reliable than another, hence to find the value of the performance of each the time spent In effecting rep•lirs or adjustments should Pe. reckoned. - There are many small powered motor cycles that can put up a creditable aver' age speed when everything is considered.

hEW BOOKS.

Societal Evolution. By A. G. Keller. London: Macmillan and Co., Ltd. Os. 6d. net.

Professor Keller attempts in this work to apply the Darwinian conceptions of variation, selection, transmission and adaptation to °society. He has been impressed by the loose- ness with which the evolutionary terminology is used in sociological writings. " Natur- ally," he says in his preface, " the terms originally used bY Darwin and his followers have suffered, in passing from hand to hand, a considerable amount of damage. Like coins that have been circulating indiscriminately, they have lost their sharpness of outline and definiteness of superscription. It is almost impossible to discover what some authors who deal with social topics—let alone the host of popular writers and orators—mean by evolu- tion." The pressing need of the present, therefore, is according to Professor Keller, to establish a science of society on an evolution- ary basis conceiving evolution to be not a cosmic philosophy, but the ceaseless operation of those factors of variation, &c:, a concep- tion which has proved of immense service in the biological sciences. Professor Keller regards his work as a con-

tinuation of that of his predecessor at Havard (Professor Sumner), and so bases-his investi- gation on the conceptions of " folkways" and " mores " put forward by Sumner. Professor Keller defines " folkways " thus: "As a natural and essential societal form the folk- way is analogous to the gertn or. embryo. It is less derived and more primordial. Folk- ways represent the lowest terms or matrix of the institutions whose aggregate, civilisation or culture is the external measure and profec-

tion qf the human type of adaptation to en- vironlent." When the ?folkways" have stood the test of long experience, and a consider- able amount of concurrence has come about with regard to them, they are taken to be con- ducive to group welfare, and after a time receive social and religious sanction and becomes "mores."

Professor Keller in the course of a fairly long analysis subjects these conceptions of the " folkways " and " mores" to the test of the Darwinian ideas. On the whole we think he

as made out his case, although we feel that

le is hardly sympathetic enough towards cer- ain aspects of his subject. His analysis of

frontier- society, as a case of adaptation, is, we think, extremely interesting and valuable, as also is his treatment of highly civilised societies.

The book is very readable, and should do mne.h to clarify our ideas, and it should_cer-

tainly give a- powerful impulse towards a more profound study of the fundamental con- ditions and forces of our social life.

FIRST YEAR OF WAR.

The King And Queen Attend Solemn Intercession Service.

The anniversary a the declaration of war by Great Britain against Germany was marked on Wednesday by a great national service of inter cession at St. Paul's Cathedral, at which the King and Queen, the Premier, and most of the Cabinet were present, and at which the Arch- bishop of Canterbury preached.

Similar services were held in every large town throughout the Country, arid in addition hmumerable 'patriotic demonstrations took place.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. BALFOUR'S CONFIDENCE

Mr. Balfour, addressing a great patriotic meeting in the London Opera House on Wed- nesday night, expressed the resolve of the nation and the Empire in a resolution which was put at all the War anniversary meetings throughout the country on Wednesday That on this anniversary of the declara- tion of a righteous war this meeting records its inflexible determination to continue to, a victorious end the struggle in mainten- ance of those ideals of liberty and justice which are the common and sacred cause of the Allies.

Mr. Balfour declared that not only was our resolve unshaken, but our confidence in the ultimate result was even more sure than it was in the earlier days of this titanic conflict. If he were asked whether we had played our part in this world-tragedy he had no hesitation in the answer he would give. To those critics who complained that we and our Dominions had done less than was expected, he replied that our land forces were growing daily in strength, and that what we had done was only part of what we were going to do. A little over twelve months ago the decision of this country seemed to hang in the balance. But the decision Came to was a right decision. It was a critical moment for civiliAtiOn, and the decision; taken by the Government of this country saved civilisation. 'But for the British Fleet he did not, believe that it would have been possible for our' Allies to have carried on the war. . Sir Robert Borden declared that the Dominion were inspired by an inflexible determination to do their part.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
DOING OUR SHARE.

Speaking at Folkestone, Mr. Bonar taw in- timated that when the time for discussing peace terms arrived the British self-governing Dominions would have a voice in the negotia- tions. He defended Britain against the sugges- tion that it was not doing its share, and de- Oared that every man and every shilling would be risked to win the war.

Speaking at Hove, Sir Edward Carson said it was idle to pretend that the situation was the same as that of one or two months ago. Even the temporary suspension of Russia's help laid a great and additional burden upon the efforts we must make. We fought the battle all through the year with her help, and we might to a large extent have to go on with- out her help. After' a reference to universal service "in the sense of being able to place every man in the country to the best advan- tage," he said there was no step Lord Kitchener asked for which he would not get within a few hours, and he hoped that such methods as were necessary would never through any political idea or purpose be postponed one hour from the time Lord Kitchener wished them.

An " Ould Ancient." 1 learn from Mr. Motinfield that the .parish clerk is a more ancient institution than I had supposed. I had previ?usly traced the office to the time of Augustine only. By-the-way, a stranger asked one of these functionaries as to his proper style or title and the old man, ap- parently indifferent to the correct description, replied, " Well, the first vicar he called me the clerk ;.then another came and he called me the virgin; the next said I were the Christian (sacristan or sexton), and now •I •be . clerk again." The parish clerk can claim a perfect continuity of office; in the ministry itself breaks might occur-T—as we have just seen, parsons might come and go—but the clerk 'went on forever. • No matter•whosoe'er, might reign, there he stood, four square to all ,the winds that blew, perhaps to all outward seem- ing now and again trimming his sails accord- ingly, somewhat after the manner of a certain vicar of Bray whose plan is hardly to be commended, and yet loyal at heart. . In "For Faith and Freedom," Sir Walter Besant has a sketch of an infirm old clerk stealing a look into the chest in the vestry of his church and "Aye, the old surplice and the old Book of Common Prayer. Ye have had a long rest; 'tis time for ye both to come out again. When the surplice is, out, the Book will stay no longer locked up. . , you bide your time; you know you will come back again; you are not in a hurry.. Even the. • clerk dies, but, you die not; you bide your time." I might dip further into a book .of my own mentioned in this column last week, but the foregoing may suffice.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

• By MARJORIE.

Women Signallers. Women with leisure, some private means, and good health, desirous of helping to defend their country in case of invasion, cannot do better than join the Women Signallers' Territorial Corps, of which Mrs. Parker, the sister of Lord Kitchener, is the commander-in-chief. The corps is now two hundred strong, with branches in Ireland, Westmorland, Hampshire and Ox- fordshire. The members practise signalling with flags, whistles, heliograph, air line, &C. They are trained in.dispatch'riding, on horseback and ordinary and motor cycles. The August camp of the corps in East Anglia is perhaps the most interesting of the year. The Nomen, all of whom belong to the leisured classes, live the rough and Spartan life of the soldier, and as they will be employed for a fortnight in conjunction with the military, their capacity for signalling work will be thoroughly tested.

A Friend of Working Girls. The late Hon. Maud Stallley retired cmly a very short time ago from the Metropolitan Asy- lums Board, after thirty years of work in con-

nection with that body: Various and extensive as were her activities, her name will be remem- bered chiefly in connection with working girls' tlubs. In 1880 she started the pioneer club at Soho, to which was attached a home for work- ing girls. Churches of all creeds, and• the Jews also; took up the movement, and there are now over sixty such clubs in the Metropolis. Miss Stanley also inaugurated the Children's holiday scheme. She was. the first woman to act as a Poor Law guardian, and for fifty years took an active part in every practical effort to elevate the lives of the working classes.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions. ' Domestic Refuse and the Fly Pest. The Health Department of the Halifax Cor- poration are sending out a notice to house- holders, calling their attention to the error of placing such stuff as waste paper, fish, and vegetables in the ashbins. The warning is well advised, for there is no doubt that much unnecessary material is. placed in the bins for collection. Soon flies will again be the plague of our lives, and it is a well-known fact that nothing attracts these, little pests more than the decomposing matter in ash-bins. From a sanitary point of view alone the value of burn- ing the materials mentioned must be obvious to all. Besides that, too, the-re can be little doubt that the cost of collection would be much reduced—a point that should go a long way with ratepayers. Prevention of Waste.

The Prime Minister has promised to give careful consideration to a memorial addressed to him by the Education Committee of the National Food Fund, urging the necessity for immediate attention being given to the subject of prevention of waste of the nation's food. The memorial points out that one large source of wastage is through the inefficient housekeep- ing of the working-class wife, and advocates the assistance of local authorities in pressing home by means of education warnings and admoni- tions which should be • issued by Government Departments. _ • Red Skin.

If your arms and hands are red from the red blood which courses through them, or from the tan of the life-giving and invigorating sun- shine, they must be healthy now and need no cure. In addition to being healthy they are stylish and indicate the good common sense of the owner. If they are red from some rash, or skin disease, purify the blood, bathe in tepid soft water and castile soap, dry thoroughly and rub in a lubricant, such as glycerine or mutton tallow. Cheese Aigrettes.

Quarter-pint of water, }oz. of butter, loz. of flour, lioz. of grated cheese, one egg, seasoning, cayenne. Put water and butter into a sauce- pan, and when boiling draw off the fire and stir in the flour carefully- and quickly, and boil for two or three minutes. Slightly cool the mix- ture, and add the egg well beaten. Stir well, and add grated cheese, seasoning and cayenne. Leave the mixture to get quite cold, and then drop one teaspoonful at a time into smoking hot fat, and fry a golden-brown colour. Drain on paper, and dish nicely with chopped parsley and grated cheese.

Among other things there is the drying of vegetables, to which the Germans have paid great attention, possibly in view of the war, as these comestibles figure largely in the food rations of the soldiers. It was only after the beginning of the war that the first vegetable preserving factory was started in this country at Alcester, and there seems no reason why the industry which is peculiarly suited for women workers should not develop. Every season im- mense quantities of fruit and vegetables go to waste for want of scientific and commercial methods of preserving them, and instead of im- porting thousands of tons of-vegetables, it would be possible, were the proper measures taken, to produce and preserve at home all required for the population. Autumn Fashions,.

The military note is not to be eliminated from autumn fashions, but it will be much less ob- trusive than last year. Simplicity will prevail in every department, for exaggeration will not be tolerated. When the war ends it is quite safe to say that there will be a drastic change in fashions, but that happy time is not yet. Autumn skirts, with a slight umbrella effect, will be very short, but these are suited only to girls in their teens. Quite elderly women, how- ever, can wear their gowns four inches off the ground, and yet not look unappropriately dressed. These short dresses make boots a neces- sity, in winter at any rate; if shoes are worn, high gaiter spats must cover the ankles. The most popular boots will probably be the very high model, with cloth or suede tops laced at the side. Colours will he very subdued, and dark blue, grey, and brown are to be most favoured.

HERE AND THERE.

Long Engagements. In some parts of West Africa the girls have long engagements. On the day of their birth they are betrothed to a baby boy a trifle older then themselves, and at the age of 20 they are married. The girls know of no other way of getting a husband, and so they are quite happy and satisfied. As wives they are patterns of obedience, and the marriages usually, turn out successes.

Feet of American Women. The American woman would be the most graceful woman in the world if she would only learn the graceful management of her feet. This was the verdict of a well-known French- man who visited New York recently and who appeared to derive considerable amuse- ment from watching the awkward walk of the New York women as illustrated by the pedei- trians on Fifth-avenue. The women were all well dressed, wearing the customary "hobble" skirt, which is said to be a past vogue, but to all appearances is supremely present, and the rather- conspicuous shoes ,which are a popular fad Of to-day. Not only does the latest critic accuse the American woman of ungainly movements, but a well-known dancing teacher, of New York, agrees most heartily with the Frenchman and goes so far as to add that 'feminine New Yorkers are sadly in need of a course in "feet discipline."

FAREWELL.

My space is short this week, and I have only room to bid farewell, a long farewell, to all my nephews find nieces. I wonder how many of you could spare me a small photograph, so that I should have some mementoes of my three very happy weeks as. your Uncle. I would send you each a photo of myself (though I am not very ornamental with my poor old bald head, and the stick I limp about with when the gout twinges), but it would be a terribly big and a rather expensive job, and besides, your old Uncle hates being photo- graphed. Well, dear boys and girls, I really must say good-bye, and, in doing so, would ask you not entirely to forget Your affectionate Undo HUBERT. P.B.—Next week's essay subject: "A train joun*y."

Raffia straw has been successfully exploited for hats, and for various accessories of the toilette—handbags among others. It has lately made its appearance in another, direction, and some very pretty parasols are shown, made en- tirely of it. Given a hot summer, these novel sunshades would doubtless have enjoyed a vogue, for they are light and very attractive, in addi- tion they are not by any means expensive. Some have a border of small flowers on the brim, others a big of roses at the top. This year the greater number of parasols designed for smart wear are decorated with flowers, mixed with ribbon bows. This decoration certainly suits the raffia parasol. The Japanese shape is generally used; and the long black handles con- trast with the colours of the straw which is very fine in texture. The flat topped sunshade, covered with tulle and flowers, is very dainty, and makes a charming finish to 43, garden party toilette.

The Court of the Belgian King and Queen. The fishing village of La Panne, where the King and Queen of the Belgians are now living with their elder son, is in close touch with the army. It is an ugly village, and was robbed of its original quaintness when it became a summer resort. A number of red and yellow brick villas were then built, in the sand appar- ently! There is one main thoroughfare, through which a tram passes, but the only sounds that break on the ears of the exiled monarchs are the wailing or the wind, the inrush of the tide, and the rumbling of the distant artillery. The encroaching sand covers up the gardens, and there are very few trees. In one of the row of six ,villas, or rather seaside cottages, the Bel- gian Royal Family is now housed.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular Language. Streets running north and south have the best health records. House Flies and Baby's •Bottle.

At the Mother-craft Exhibition at the Pass- more Edwards Settlement, Dr. Raiford Ross lectured on "Children and the Fly Peril." He showed by means of lantern slides how prolific and how übiquitous the fly is. There was an exhibit of a mother fly and the huge family she produced in twenty-four days thickly crowded on three fly papers. Another showed a group of worms whose eggs are carried by the fly, and which reach maturity when they have found their way from the food where the ,fly :deposits them in the human intestine. There was an exhibition of feeding bottles, one of which had a teat .that had caused the death of a baby by coming off and sticking in his throat, the flow of the milk helping to push ,it down. The correct feeding bottle with a firm teat, which encourages the baby's jaws to work, was shown. Another useful exhibit was a saucepan contain- ing six medicine bottles filled with milk, with cotton-wool corks, and water in the pan as far up as the milk, ready for sterilising by boiling. A baby's meals for a day were also shown, to give mothers an idea of quantity and quality.

Hints for Baby's Bath. The temperature for the bath is determined by age; but it is necessary that it should be given to infants and quite little children in a warm room, protected with screens from draughts. Also, there should be plenty of water, in order that the little body should be completely covered and not left with a con- siderable proportion above the level of the warm water to feel by contrast the cold. The temperature of an infant's bath should be from 95 deg. ' . to 100 deg. Fahr. and this must be tested always with a bath thermometer. This is a point which cannot be too strongly in- sisted upon, as cases of harm occur from the delusion that a nurse is able to gauge the temperature accurately in any other way. Also—and a most important point—a little cold water should always be put in the bath first. Neglect of this simple precautron has, led to trouble, for a layer of hotter water may easily get left at the bottom of the bath when the temperature of that above has been satis- iifactor,ily regulated, with the result that the unfortunate .baby 'is put down, into water of possibly scalding heat. This can never occur if the "cold water first" rule is religiously followed. By the end of the first month the temperature should have dropped to 95 deg., and from then onwards must be gradually lowered until at six months it is 90 deg. Fahr. —"Queen."

CHILDREN'S CAMP.

Striking Improvement In The Condition Of The Children.

Happy, well dressed and healthy looking boys to the number of 141 left Warrington for the Prestatyn Children's Summer Camp ' last Saturday and arrived at their destination ready for the good dinner that pas awaiting them at 12.30.

One of the most satisfactory features of the camp is the striking improvement that has taken place in the general appearance of the children who have been in camp compared with their condition in the first few years of the camp's existence. The children are well cared for, the parents evidently being desirous or doing their, best for the children. This year the bOys were under the care and protection of Wm. Farrar (superintendent), ably assisted by the following officers: J. H. Wainwright, F. Appleton, J. B. Weir, Wm. Turner, Thos. Oxley and Ernest Parsons. • Sunday was spent by -walking to church, and later swimming and games. Monday morning there was a grand walk over the mountain side; followed by a terrible thunderstorm with heavy rain; in the evening the boys were the artistes at a grand concert, at which the programme was as follows : ! Songs,.. E. Bailey, L. Eastens, W. Garrett; recitations, W. Garrett, E. Dunning, W. ' Atherton, L. Eastens', W. Millington, T. ' Birchall. The boys joined heartily in all the latest- choruses: A novel feature of the enter- tainment was an impersonation of Charlie Chaplin, by Tom Roberts, assisted by W. Garrett. "God Bless the Prince of Wales" and the National Anthem were sung in con- clusion.

Tuesday, the favourite walk to Dyserth Falls and round the Nut Groves was greatly enjoyed. Wednesday, the boys went to the village shopping, and revelled in the joy of buying and eating rock, 'toffee' and fruit. All had their photos taken, and about 80 boys bought one. Thursday was sports days. The boys col- lected from visitors for prizes, and trades- people also assisted in the gift of small prizes. _ _ _

- The events were: 80 yaids- fiat under 10, 3 prizes; 100 yards fiat over 10, 3 prizes; 3-legged race under 10, 6 prizes; 3-legged race over 10, 6 prizes; wheelbarrow race under 10, 6 prizes; wheelbarrow race over 10, 6 prizes; marble and spoon race under 10, 3 prizes; marble and spoon race over 10, 3 prizes; long distance race (handi- capped), 4 prizes. Friday was, spent in cleaning up, and also what is one of the most enjoyable items in the week's holiday, buying presents to take home to little sisters and brothers.

All returned to Warrington in the best of health at 9.45 last night, after one of the best of holidays.

"SIMILAR TO A CRYSTAL."

• The idea of citizenship is very similar to a crystafi. it changes according to the position from which we view it. At present everything is seen from the point of view of the war. We cannot see a trench dug for a gas main in the street withciat clothing it with armed men; every special train we imagine to be packed with soldiery,. and the waggons that go along the lines are filled in fancy-with mines, or gun- carriages, or the like. This is as it should be. A nation that is waging seven or eight cam- paigns, each wieh its deathroll of thousands,

cannot be indiffezent or callous to the warlike sights around it. We must expect some effect on the spirit of the nation, some repayment for the thousand' sacriffms made, and made se will- ingly, too. The French Revolution was a cruel business, and! the horror of it has set an the world against such turnioils in the future. Any reader of the "'Tare of Two Cities" will re- member how vividly Dickens pictures: the fanatic populace coming to the grindstone in the early morning,. light, and whetting their knives for fresh carnage. Blood! blood! was the incessant cry, and the guillotine exacted toll of all that was gaod' in the intellectual life of France. Yet, when an this is said, and due reckoning made' for it, it must be remembered that the Revolution left France a better country, with more freedom fOr-thaught and action, and put an end for ever to the days of serfdom. It is a law of the universe, as immutable as the raw of the indestructibility of matter, that no true sacrifice shall go without its reward; this is ffre "eternal Pole-star" above the destinies of men. The spirit that inspires a whole nation to give up luxury for potato bread, the spirit that has filled the ranks of our volinitary army, in which many of the men have enlisted with the perfect conviction that they will never leave it alive,. this spirit is going•to have a say in the motif& ing of the future. The army indeed is the most democratic organism tiiat was ever brought into being; in it The soldier, the King, and the peasant Are working together as one, and it is brimming over with: comradeship, the fellowship of which Morrie said that in it was: life; and lack of it was death. Peril of I hternecihe. Strife.

It was time indeed that this. spirit should re- turn to the earth. Our weakness is shown by the hesitancy of Parliament in entering upon the war. Civil war in Irelitntf, tabour struggles at home—these are no proud trophies on the escutcheon 'of a nation. It' is only now, indeed, after being at war for months; that we are get,- ting our manufactories in hand, and putting an end to the internecine war oft'capital and labour, a war which has been called' "as inconceivable as the habit the man of the rower classes has of breaking the furniture when, he quarrels with his wife." The present war dbes at least give scope to the ingenuity of the combatants. But the old Struggle, in which splendid machines were left idle because their introduction would' lead to a strike, in which trade was jealous, of trade and combine jealous of combine, this was. a reversion to the days of the ord crofters. rt was not to the interestsof 'progress, in industry or in economics, it was bad production and bad! syndicalism. These blind' struggles, these at- tempts to put back the clbck of civilisation, these, we may hope, will become part of the mysterious anomalies of the past. Life is too short:

Life and death alik& ye do forget

In needless strife, until'on 6'olllo sure day Death takes your scarcery tasted life away. And the chiefs of industry also had much to be proud of, much that showed their judg,iftent and foresight. Engineering•and chemistry, the two forces which, given a free hand, have made their name awful on the frontiers of France and Russia, tearing up the face of the land in parts like some cataclysm of nature, these were given mere ancillary tasks in• the short-sighted search for wealth at any price. "The Primrose Path ef Decadence." We can see now how easily we were making for the primrose path of decadence, following in the steps of Carthage and Venice. We were gone too far to be recalled lay oratory or prayers, such drastic lesson was necessary as has opened our eyes to-day. We have learnt the paramount need of organisation, or co-ordinated effort, of harmonious work. Everything is so involved nowadays, that a single war brings into the field the whole power of the Old World. We are only just realising how powerful we are, and how vast ' our resources. So pre-occupied were we with the thought that we were the mother- country, that we failed to see that the colonies were grown-up children. In imperial rnattert also we had not kept pace with civilisation. If indeed we really learn the lesson of the war, perhaps it will not have been too costly. An army is a mighty machine; roads, railways, bridges, towns are a/I called into being for its progress. There is no time for the foolish de- bate that wears on the days of peace. Action, action on a gigantic scale, action seemingly reckless of all cost, these are the means that carry it on its way. What if, after the war, we enrolled an army whose mission was production, not destruction? What if we enrolled an army

for .agricultare? What if we did our work in brigades, instead of in the present pettyfogging way? What if we had a "live" minister for the munitions of peace, and gave science all the re- sources necessary for her research? This is the kind of arrangement that will have to come, and the sooner we forestall it the better. And the Empire of which we are all so proud, which has shown itself so firmly established after the labours of men like Cromer, who gave the best of their lives to further it—we have been chal-

lenged, "By what right do you hold that mighty Empire," and have.entered the lists to defend our claim.

Less Parochial; More Imperial Government.

But the right will not be established when the guns have ceased their thunder throughout Europe. Government will have to be less parochial and more imperial; there will have to be less talk of what pheasants feed on, and more talk of what men feed on. Oh! the old forms of government have weathered through many a heavy storm, and it is no use throwing them overboard in the wild frenzy of the ideal. There is a deal of room in them for expansion, when once they have been vivified and breathed into with new life. The spirit and not the form, the ideal at which we are aiming and not so much the material with which we are working, are the esiential features. How discomforted. are the old pessimists who told us in many a weary Jeremiad that the race was effete and that the first rude test would shatter it. The mask of civilisation and cosmopolitanism has been shaken away, and the type of English manhood has appeared again---lean, clean-limbed and virile: "We cannot help being cheerful, when we are so fit." This is the result of six months' training; what could we do if vte could keep men so fit in peace? It is not Eugenics, or racial selection, or any other artificiality that men want, but good hours, good food, good quarters. We can all of us ask, like Lady Macbeth, "Who would have thought that the old man had so much blood in him?" - _ - -

But it is hard work to try and idealise war.

There are sad hearts in Europe to-day, and there will be many more ere this business is at an end.

DUTIES OF CerlZENSMP.—Continued:

It would not have been so bad if war had been fought cleanly, if men had always been chivalrous, if leaders had always been just, The employment of such a weapon as poison gas, with its inevitable reprisals, and the growing intro-. duction of savagery, one side vying with the other, give a pang to the heart that would have relied on steel against steel, and shell against shell. People will be loth to loose the dogs-of war for many a year henceforth. The Legacy of the Generations to Come.

We are living on the wealth that should have been our children's inheritance, and, glihy as we talk of immediate sacrifices-, we rarely think how long those sacrifices will be continued. The consequences will be felt by many a generation. We have been feeding guns instead of men, and there vrill need to be hard work &tie to make up the deficit. Our wealth is slipping through our hands like water, men are falling before the sickle in thousands, and the whole Empire wilt have to join -hands if w'e are net to be broken by the load. It is our best who are falling- on those foreign battle fields, and" we shall'have to take care of what remains for many a year, unable to waste life in slums and courts as we did of old. We must be sparing- of par- simonious with it, misers of it: Yet if events show that the "wild beast of force" still-courses with the blood through our veins, they have shown also how much that Is good is in man- kind. We have been working in terms of thousands, yet always the individual has dis- played his own characteristics, the peculiar nature that distinguishes him from the rest of the world. Surely it is not an impossible task to reconcile individualism with collectivism? to have each individual perfectly developed on the best side of his nature, and yet to escape the lack of common purpose that overthrew the Greeks; to organise the indiViduala to work in great bodies without the destruction of that in- dividuality of theirs? This war has at least taught us that nothing is impossible when a nation sets • its mind to it. If we 'learn this lesson of common purpose. and throw away all the childish discords, the ruture will be bright and not black.

Great hail we cry to the corners From the dazzling unknown shore; Bring us hither your sun and your summers. And renew us the world as of yore; You shall teach us your songs and new numbers And things that we dreamed not before. Yea, in spite of a dreamer who slumbers And a singer who, sings no more.

A Canned Joke.

Once when liudyard Kipling was a boy he ran out on the yardarm of a ship.

"Mr..Kiplingl." yelled a seared sailor, "your boy is on a yardarm, and if he lets go he'll drown!"

_ " Ah !" responded Mr. Kipling, with a yawn; "but he won't let go." This incident also happened to Jim Fiske, Horace Walpole, Napoleon Bonaparte, Dick Turpin, Julius CEesar sand the poet Byron. Presently it will " happen " again, for canned jokes, like clothes pins, are useful until lost.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY. AUGUST 7, 1915.

WARSAW.

The Polish capita! has4fallen into the hands of the enemy, but the brilliant 'generalship of, the GRIND DUKE NICHOLAS has saved the Russian Army to fight another daY and upon more' even terms. It is sad to think that Germany has won OTeil this transitory sac- -cowl, for it means lengthening out the struggle with more useless loss of life. , Apart from this consideration the possession of Warsaw does not Count for much. It is, in fact. worse than useless to the Germans, if, as we believe, the effort to get there has robbed them of hundreds of thousands of men whom they cannot repla". The Allies have entered upon the second year of war this week in good heart, and in the certainty of final and com- plete success.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
NO SENIOR FOOTBALL.

It will be seen from a paragraph on our sports page that the Warrington Football Club have given their final answer those too-enthusiastic supporters of the NOrthern Union League, who, having failed to get the clubs to carry on" business as usual " during the war, . have attempted a flanking move- ment with the object of merely substituting friendly for league matches. The Warrington Club were the first to say that they would abandon the game for all- except juveniles, and they carried their point to this extent: that the League, as a league, has been placed in a state of suspended animation, to came to

_

full life again in happier times- Now they have been approached and asked whether they would be willing te play friendly matches. As will be seen from the resolu- tion passed this week, the committee are sticking to the spirit as well as the word a their previous resolve. They will have noth- ing whatever to do with senior football for the remainder of the period of the war- On the contrary,, they are on the point of arrang- ing a programme of schoolboy league and in- termediate league fixtures for Wiklerspool. Football spectators may therefore Make up their minds that during the coming winter they will be privileged to see the juniors at play—and as they know, the boys very often give really clever exhibitions. We shall know 344 soon as the school holidays are over what shape this programme will actually assume. In the meantime We should like to bespeak the support of the public for the Football Club for the clear and patriotic stand they have made.. There are certain charges to be met whatever happens.

THE LATE NH. ROBSON.

These who are familiar with that school classic, "Tom Brown's Schooldays," will re- member how Tom, some years after he had left Rugby, learnt by chance while on holi- days of his old master's death. They will remember the profoundly sorrowful impres- sion the news, thoughtlessly shouted across to him by a frienil, who was reading a news- paper, madb.upon the old Rugby boy : " Hello, Brown/ here's something for you. Arnold, of Rugby, is dead." To the one it was a bit of idle news; to the other it was one of the vital things of life. The man who had moulded/ Tom's character for this earth and pointed him the way for the neat was no more, and Tom's holiday was ended. The news that Mr. Robson, head master of our Second- ary School is dead, will be learnt by many of his pupils, past and present, during the pres- ent holiday season in some such manner as the intimationregarding Arnold came to Brown in Scotland. All will regret the passing of a capable master, who implanted more of his nature into them than they will ever know, the man who did much to raise their school, of which they are proud, to its present high level, and who, but for the terrible handicap of a dread disease, had it in his power to do very much more. During his illness the school has had the priceless advantage of two con- scientious and successful workers in Mr. Broome, Mr. Robson's chief assistant, and Miss Clarke, the principal lady teacher,, who have been largely responsible for maintaining the school at Mr. Robson' state of perfec- tion.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SELLING AND COLLECTING

NUISANCE.

Not before it was time has the Watch Committee taken steps to clear the Streets of a nuisance to which they have long been sub- ject. With the• best intentions in do; world many people have set out to sell and to beg for various war funds; with the worst inten- tions many have imitated them. Even where the unauthorised person undertakes the work with honest intentions, the multiplication of the appeals that are made upon one as one passes along the streets has, in itself, become a source of annoyance. When there is a danger that the " proceeds " are more than likely to be applied to the personal advan- tage of the seller or beggar, the annoyance is very much aggravated. We are not sur- prised to hear that complaints have been -received by the Watch Committee of " collec- tions" in the streets by unauthorised persons and of promiscuous selling of articles and col- lecting in the streets by school children; and we note with pleasure that the committee have resolved:

That the Town Clerk issue a notice requesting the public not to contribute to any War Fund except to persona authorised to collect by this committee, and forbidding school children, to sell articlea or to collect money in the streets.

THE OBDURATE HEALTH COMMITTEE.

No headway has been made in the dispute between the Education Committee and the Town Council respecting the claim of the former to have a joint voice with the Health Committee in the appointment of officials to carry out the health duties of the authorities in the schools. At a recent meeting of ,the Health Committee the matter was discussed with the Chairman of the Education' Corn-. mitteo and the Director; but it was still resolved:

That it is ia the interest of the public health of the town that the appointments be made by the Health Committee as at present, and that it is inadvisable to make any change.

Mr; Kingdon has threatened to resign if the Rea4th Committee persisted in this stand;

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 7, 1915.

OUR FIRST D.C.M.

in the "London Gazette," the first local 'Derritorial honour of this kind to do so. The official notice says:—

1981, Private J. EATON, 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment For conspicuous gallantry on 16th June, 1915, at Hooge, in remaining in a support trench for one hour under heavy fire dig- ging out a comrade who had been buried by shell-fire.

This gallant act took plainon the day of the great charge in circumstances we have already reported at length.

THE DEED THAT WON REWARD.

Private Eaton was in one of the trenches where the enemy's shells were falling thickest. Five men near him were either killed outright or mortally wounded and a sixthr-Diummer Peter Wright—was alreast completely buried under a heavy fall of earth which followed the explosion of the self-same shell. Private Eaton hoard the half-buried young drummer calling for help and, without a moment's hesitation seized a trenching tool and started to dig him out. Before he heard the Drummer's cries he had moved to a lessexposed part of the trench, and despite the fact that the spot which he had left was open to the enemy's - fire—the parapet having been blown away—be ran back and dug away for over an hour and, eventually was able •to haul his comrade out of what must other- wise have proved his grave. When the Drummer was at length extricated it was found that he had been badly injured in the right arm. His rescuer saw him safely bestowed after his terrible ordeal, and sub- sequently the injured lad was sent • across to England and was treated in a military hospital at Sheffield. As Private Eaton worked away with his trenching tool the German gunners found the range of the trench so well that, by the time he had finished his task, breastworks had been practically swept level.

"TERRIERS" EXCELLENT

WORK.

Commanding Officer Congratu- lated By Staff Officers.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A CHAT WITH COLONEL

FAIRCLOUGH.

Colonel Breretou Fairciough. who commands the Warrington Territorials, has recently been home on short leave. His appearance at the presentatioa to, Corporal Tombs, V.C., at the Town Hail on Saturday night, was the first in- timation of his presence in the town that all save the Colonel's family and a few of his more intimate friends had., The Colonel was looking remarkably fit and well, and needless to say he had a cordial welcome both from the waiting crowd and from the Mayor and the other civic dignitaries who were assembled iaside the build- ing and on the steps. On Wednesday—the day before he returned to duty—the Colonel consented to give a brief interview to an "Examiner" representative, hiring first stipulated that he was not to be asked any details about the actual fighting, ,

===== 0002601_19150807_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
STREET SIGHT THAT SURPRISED THE

COLONEL.

"I must say," he said, "that I was 'very much surprised when I wandered through the streets of.' Warrington on Monday to see the number of young men knocking about,' and not yet in khaki—young men who appeared to be eminently fitted to play a more important part for the country generally than they are apparently doing at the present time. I was told that a good many of the young men I speak of might be employed on munitions work, but I don't see that that altogether explains their appatent slackness away, for if those young men got inside the Yarious forces of the Crown, they would make it possible for many older men, who are at present more or less un- profitably employed, to take. up monitions work.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT "TOMMY" THINKS OF THE "SLACKER."

"1 want you to say this!" proceeded the Colonel. "If the young unmarried men would only realise what a strong feeling there .is con- cerning their `slacking amongst the troops abroad, they would, for their own credit's sake, lose no, time in getting inside the ranks. Aye, if they heard what the 'Townies' out there

are saying about their apparent want of patriotism, all the single young fellows would be covered with shame. If it could be brought home to them, how they are shirking their re- sponsibility, they would enlist straight away, or never dare again to lift their heads, in public. 'D —d slackers!' is bow 'Tommy' in the trenches talks of them; they make him sick, he says! •

===== 0002601_19150807_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE MEN ARE SPLENDID

"As regards the Battalion," said the Colonel, in reply to a question, "all I will say is that the men are splendid, and I don't think I could pay them a higher compliment than that. It is really an honour to have command of tem. They are keeping, very fit, indeed; and they are in most excellent spirits, and there is not one of them but is looking forward to the time when he can 'get into' the enemy and finish the job off. "I should like to express the thanks of the Battalion." the Colonel continued, "for the many kindnesses which have been extended to them by the people of Warrington and dis- trict., The lavish way in which ,they have sea gifts out to us is very creditable i,ndeed, and ,it puts heart into .the chaps to know that they are being remembered in the old town. There is a committee of officers, and commissioned and non-commissioned officers' wives, as well as of ladies interested in the Battalion generally, working at headquarters on comforts and neces- saries for, the men of the Battalion. They are sending out parcels rewlarly, and they contri- bute very largely to the comfort, welfare and contentment of the men.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
"A VERY HARD TIME INDEED!"

"We have had a very hard time, indeed, out yonder,", the Colonel concluded, "and the men of the Warrington Territorial Battalion have done their share of much of the most excellent work that has been done. Of course, we have suffered losses in the doing of it, but that's all in the day's routine. I have been complimented not once, but many times, by staff officers on the excellent work that has been accomplished liy the men of my Battalion."

Mark Twain Caught Out. A poet, a pair of trousers across his knee, sat in his attic •trying to thread a needle. "The proper way," he said, "is to hold tho thread still and push the ineedle toward it, or to hold the needle still and push the thread toward it. Mark Twain says both ways are right; then he says they are both wrong." Taking down "The Prince and the Pauper," the poet read: - -

"'fie did as men have always done, and prob- ably always will do to the end of time—held the needle still and tried to thrust the thread through the eye, which is the opposite of a woman's way."

Then the poet, taking down Huckleberry Finn, read

"Bless you, child, when you set out to thread a needle, don't hold the thread still and fetch the needle up to it; hold the needle still, and poke the thread at it—that's the way a woman 'most always does; but ttman always does Vothet:

===== 0002601_19150807_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
'4 GOD BLESS HIM."

Drummer peter Wright wrote home to his sister, Mrs. Hilton, describing his narrow escape

" ' coal-box "he said. "dropped into our trench and buried six of us when it exploded. I was the only one of the half-dozen to come out alive, the other five poor chaps being sufio- cated. As I lay underaeath the soil I prayed, and my prayer was heard. A chap named Joe Eaton must have heard ut y cries, for he started _

digging, and after about- an hour and three- quarters' work, he dug me out. May God bless him and bring him home safe as a reward for his brave deed, and it was a brave one, for as he worked to extricate me shells were whizzing past, and some only missed us by inches...." Lance-Corporal Eaton., who is 28 years of age in November, is the sea of Mr. Jos. Eaton, Orford. Prior to going to the Front he made his home with his brother-ie-law and sister, Mr. and Mrs. Hughes, at 23, Dickenson-street. He is a Firth Mattress Ca.'s employe. Drummer Wright is 23 years of age. He joined the old Volunteers when eleven years of age, and continuing to serve when the Terri- torial Battalion was constituted. He made his home with his brother-in-law and sister. Mr. and Mrs. John Hilton, at 64„ Scott-street. Both rescuer and,reseeed are National School "old boys."
