"ENOUGH SAW."

Writing on Sunday, July 27th, in response to a letter from a relation, congNatulating him on his achievement, Lance-Corporal Heaton wrote :—" Thanks very much for the congratu- ,lations, and let me tell you that I did not blush, neither did I feel funny. lam ~not nervous, and I never will be either. It W3S presented to me by General —. I have, however, only got the ribbon yet, but I will get the medal when I come to England, and if I get killed, Clara would get it, but, of course you know, I shan't get killed, so enough said." Mrs. Fairclough, wife of Colonel Fairclough, commanding the Warrington' Territorials,. has recently visited Lance-Corporal Eaton's sister, Mrs. Hughes, and congratulated her upon her brother's fine achievement.

MERIT THE PRAISE THEY GOT!

" Terriers " Bore Themselves Bravely In The Bio Charge.

Writing to a friend Private S. V. Podmore, of the Warrington Territorials, says; "I am get- ting fit again after my misfortune in getting 'bowled out' on June 16th. I am expecting to rejoin my regiment at any moment now. I see from the papers that the %boys' are having leave. They thoroughly deserve it, for they bore themselves bravely throughout the big at- tack in which they figured. They merit every bit of praise which was bestowed upon them by the Brigadier General."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
AVat Letters and Photographs.

We invite our readers to submit to us letters received from their soldier friends. We shall also be glad to receive photo- graphs of soldiers who have been killed, wounded, mentioned in dispatches or other- wise become a source of interest to the public.

The letters will be carefully edited so that nothing will appear in print that ought not to appear.

The photographs, when suitable, will be published in " The Examiner," which has an esta'flished reputation as a picture paper. All letters and photographs are returned after use, undamaged, to the owners.

LEADING SEAMAN F. L. WILSON.

nurses are very kind. they will do anything for you. It is such a change to being in the trenches. We get plenty of cigarettes, and any amount to eat, and every comfort! There are seven °there of our battalion in my ward,

so I am not lonely. I am about frre miles ouf.: side Cairo. It is a lovely piacd, with palm trees all round. You can see the Pyramids from the hospital windows; it would do your eyes good to be 4ere for a week or two. - ` I have lost ray pack again. and I had a lot of curios insit ready to bring home with me. I had two Turkish shells and a lot of 'smaller things, but L may have another chance to get BOMO more.

" I was wounded in a bayonet charge, so I had not much chance to pick up my belong- lugs."

Native Of Newcastte.

Leading-Seaman Wilson, who is single and aged 28, was borq in Newcastle-on-Tyne, a-nd lived there untill five years ago, when the family cape to live in Warrington. He then entered the employment of Messrs. Hanson and Edw-aids, irimfoUnders, as a blacksmith; and Later as a draper for Messrs. Brown Bros., of Bewsey-street. At the outbreak of war he had just joined the employment, in a similar capacity, of Compton House, Liverpool. He, however, relinquished his position to join the Nelson Battalion of the Royal Naval Division.

It will be remembered that " The Examiner" gave u special interview with Leading-Seaman Wilson immediately after the celebrated fight and evacuation of the trenches at Antwerp— which was the first active service he had seen. For the Da.rdanelles he set out early in Feb- ruary—on what ship is not known—and during the last few months has taken part in most of the terrible fighting round what has been called " the gates of Hell." Only about six of his many letters have been received by his relatives.

Two Escape's.

A remarkable point about Leading-Seaman

Wilson's departure was that he was sent ashore just before his vessel was sunk by an enemy torpedo. He also sailed on H.M.S. Triumph, but did not happen to be on board when it, too, was sunk by the enemy. The seaman formerly attended Ellesmere- street Sunday School.

"OUT WITH THE BOMB THROWERS."

How Private " Jack " Cartwright Received Ills Wounds.

Mrs. Cartwright, of 27, Dixon-street, War- rington, has received from a chum of her son's a letter anxiously inquiring as to how he is getting on. The son in question, Private "Jack" Cartwright, is in the 2nd South Lancs., and is at present lying in an English military hospital recovering from a bad wousd in the foot.

The friend—Private P. M'Culloch—writes:— "I was Jack's chum; we used to have our meals together. The day .he got wounded he .wae,out with the bomb throwers. I heard that he had been hit, but I do not know' how he is going on. We have been having a stiff time of it out here—shot and shell .flying in all direc-. tions! ... We all took an interest in Jack!"

PRISONERS OF WAR. Friday Morning's Post At The

Parr Hall.

As to the necessity for the continuation of the forwarditg of parcels of food to the prisoners of war in Germany, the following cards speak for themselves:—

' "Having heard that you are sending parcels of food to the prisoners of war belonging to the South Lancashire Regiment, I would ,be thankful if you could send a small parcel of food to me at your earliest convenience. I have no one in England to send me anything, only my widowed mother, and she can't afford to send me much out of her small allowance.—l remain, yours, Pte. Thomas Smith, 6692, S.L.R." "Dear Sir,—Please excuse me for having taken the liberty of writing, but owing to being a prisoner of war for the past eight months I am sorely in need, having no friends to whom I can write for assistance. I Would be very thankful if you could forward me a few provisions.—I remain in anticipation, 1850, Arthur Pennington, 3rd S.L.R.'!

Fatal Pneumonia.

During the week ending July ,31st there were in Warrington N births against 33 in the corre- sponding week last year. There were 25 deaths against 18 last year. No fewer than eight of the deaths were due to pneumonia, three were from consumption and one from scarlet fever. The new cases of scarlet fever numbered 24, bringing the total in hospital up to 117.

"Wild Goose Chase" For Fire Brigade:

A neighbour who mistook for a conflagration, a flame caused by a boilermau raking ent the fire in the boiler at Messrs. Fleming's Tannery, Fennel-street, at a late hour on Monday night, gave an alarm at the Warrington Fire Station, whereupon the Superintendent and a full-com- plement of men turned oat with the motor fire engine, only to find that the alarm iraa a false one.

Aecident To Rev. J. Yonge.

We regret to hear that the Rev. John Tenge met with an aecident on Saturday evening. As many of our readers will know, the Elles- mere-road side of the Ship Canal has many fine shrubs, and, some mischievous boys were at the time attempting to set some of them oa.fire. The reverend gentleman tried to persuade the boys to desist, but finding such an effort useless endeavoured to climb the railings to stop them. In doing so he fell and broke his left wrist. Dr. Langdale was at once sum- moned and Mr. Youge is now, we are glad to say, makiag good progress under his care.

4. Warrington-Canadians' Silver Wedding.

A large body of Warringtonians will be keenly interested to know that on Juno 26th Mr. and Mrs. James Barlow, formerly of Tanner's-lane, Warrington, and caretakers of the Bewsey-road, Weslf3yan Church, celebrated their silver wedding in Canada, whither they migrated a couple of years ago. On the date in question Mr. and Mrs. Barlow, their family and family connections, sat down to tea at their home. , 2.15, Glendale-avenue, Hamilton, Ontario, and many choice silver presents and compliments were even and extended to the host and hostess. After tea the party assembled at Farr's Hall. Mr. and Mrs. Robert Cade, former caretakers of, Silver-street School, Warrington, were of the company, and Mr. Cade occupied the chair. About 126 attended, including many Warringtonians, who enjoyed the programme of music and entertainment provided'. Mr. and Mrs. Barlow expressed their warm appreciation of the manner in which their friends had recognised the occasion, and the guests replied by cheering them and wish.: ing them much continued happiness. Mr. E. Horton took a flashlight photograph of the party.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling For Local Charities.

The annual bowling match between the War- rington Caledonian Association and the Works' Bowling Association, fixed for August 21st, is ,this year to be considerably enlarged in form, in the interests of three local charities, viz.: The Warrington Infirmary, the Nursing Association, and the Children's Summer Camp. The Warrington Cricket Club have put the whole of their ground at the disposal of the committee responsible for the arrangements. A variety entertainment will be given, and a con- cert by the members of the Brooklyn • Glee Party. The Buffaloes Band will be in attend- ance and will play for dancing. The amuse- ments will include Aunt Sallies and cocoa-nut shies. Mrs. H. Greened' will perform the opening ceremony. The committee consists of representa- tives from the Caledonian Association, the Works' Bowling Association, the Infirmary Board of Management, the Working Men's Infirmary Committee, the Nursing Association, the Children's Summer Camp Committee, the Warrington Cycling Club and the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club. The officials are: Chairman, Mr. 3. S. Davenport (president of the Caledonian Association), Mr. W. Murray (vice-chairman), Mr. 3. Tennant (Caledonian Association) and Mr. W. Patrick (Works' .Bowling Association) are the honorary' secre- taries.

More Trippers.

The railway companies, locally at any rater were even busier than is customary on Bank Holiday, for despite the fact that. there were no cheap tripe, the number of bookings was excess of last year's total—a pretty good proof of the wonderful prosperity of Warrington in' these times of war. At the Cheshire Lines Station, for instance, the takings were practi- cally £2Oll more than last year, whilst the num- ber if passengers carried was much larger, the only difference being that the journeys under- taken by most of the trippers were not se long as ordinarily.

Temperance League. At last night's meeting of the Warrington and District Citizens' Temperance League, Mr:Dale presided, and remarked that drink robbed chil- dren of clothes and food and comfort. 114. added that those who pleaded now for temper- ance included some of the greatest personalities of the times.—The speaker for the evening (Mr. Mecum, of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of llope Union) emphasised the need for the - spending of money only on necessaries, as after the war, when the munition works would not be prcrriding nearly so much employment, and the country .would be inundated with returned soldiere, we should feel the pinch very seyerely. —As a mark of the fact that it was Bknil of Hope Union night, a solo was rendered by Miss Ada !lamer and a recitation Was giyen by Visa Elliott.

THE NEARER THE BETTEit

When Distance Does Not Lend

Enchantment.

Corporal Charles Seymour, of the 4t.103att.. of the King's Liverpool Regiment (S4hinfl Brigade; Lahore Di-vision) writing to his `wife, Mrs. E. Seymour, who lives at 19, Edirard- street, Warrington.. says :—" We have just -tome out of the trenches, where we have been for eighteen-da,ve. And a rough.time we hare 'hag' of It—ehelled every day I. . A shell Cams into the nest traverse to me and killed fivtai outright; indeed, two of the poor fellows were picked up in Flews. Billeted In An Old Convent

"We would rather be twenty-five yards iron the Germans than, say, four hundred, as the farther you are away the more shells you get The last lot of trenches we were in were only fifty yards away from the enemy, with the result that all 'we' had to put up with we're e, few bombs and trench-mortars. . . ===== 0002601_19150807_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
IN TRENCHES NINE MONTHS. Stockton Heath Man's Fine Record 01

Service. Sergeant Arthur Jones, of the Ist Cheshire ' Regiment, who has been slightly wounded in the left knee, in the course of a letter written from No. 8 Stationary Hospital, France, to ,a friend, Mr. W. W. Watkins, 2, Heath-street, StOckton Heath, says :—"Just a. few lines. to heartily thank you for the "Examiner," :and to let you know that I am going on all right. The wound is now almost healed, and the reason that it has taken so long is because was thoroughly run down after my long stay in the thing line. .. lam here to pick up all I can in the way of strength and weight for the coming winter. I am trying to forget everything fur the present, and am indulging in sun baths, and nice walks along the cliffs. We can see Dever unite plainly here on a ekes day, and of COilrdb the Huns' pirate boats are- ' always knocking about. We can hear the guns, so you see we are not out of action altogether. I cannot say when I will be leaving here, but !I am not grumbling, for I eat and sleep well, and what is more I have earned all the rest 1 get, even if tho war lasts until Christmas, for nine months in the trenches without a sick report, leave, or anything else, I am sun, yos will agree is not a bad record."

THE NORTH SEA AND THE CHANNEL. "Jngland .has now bottled 'up the North Sea 47 its 'command of the British Channel. It will be necessary in future to re-establish a Lars liberum (a free sea). GERMANY AND TURKEY.

th.2-lernlany has been for about thirty-fiTe years te; associates of Turkey in developing Turkish sk,„ll°l7, commerce and industry. - THE COMMERCE OF THE WORLD. ',Ger 4 , thit' • InanY stands, and has always sloot4 for oPen. door and equal opportunity policy

as to China and to other eolantries as well as to the British colonies, and it must be, strictly maintained. All such underhanded proceedings as, for instance, the Japanese have reslarted to, attempting to throttle foreign commerce by the possession of the railways in Manchuria, must be done away with, and all the Powers must see to it that no more parts of the earth are closed, to the exclusive advantage of any one nation. While every nation must have an -undisputed right to treat foreign goods and foreign immigrants as she sees fit in her own interests, every nation must treat all other nations in a spirit of equality and without die- criraination.

THE FATE OF THE SMALLER NATIONS. "Of course, it is incumbent upon Germany to see that, sueh as have helped her in her struggle shall not be left to the mercy of her antagonists. The right of the peoples to frame their own destinies must be fully recognised. If the Finnish nation, which is of non-Slavic elesoent, allow% to join their Swedish brethren, we -will have to stand up for them. If Poland has the necessary vitality, she should have a chance to show it. If the 14oers want to be in- dependent they should have that right. And if Egypt wants to return to Turkey she must be permitted to do so. All this must be done in such a way that no new dangers Can arise to the dual ethanol?.

There is nothing in this programme that would seriously e,hange the aspect of Europe. There is no wish for worl'd-dominion, or any unduly predominant .power in western Europe incommensurate with the mass of 12.2,4100,000 of Garman. and Austrians, and there is no danger to the peace of Europe. It is simply the carry- jag eut of the peaoeful aims that Germany has had for the last forty-four years—the only nation of Europe that, even in the face of in- tense provocation, has never let herself be dragged into any war, or has taken bylbree fact of territory against the will of the owner. "In conclusion, I will say that I feel 'sure ,that I am at one with the best German ele- ment, .and that my opinions are shared by almost everybody in my country."

THRIFT CAMPAIGN.

Warrington Committee's Pre- liminary Appeal To The People.

A year's war lias taught tie that Its fatally underestinaated tire military power of the German people and their capacity for sacrifice and concentrated dettrmination. _

Germany is fighting to gratify her ambition. We are fighting for our existence. We must realise that victory lies in men, munitions and money. _ . .

The great national thrift movement is not concerned directly with recruiting, or with the supply ef munitions, but with furnishing the silver bullets, also essential to success: If the war is to be maintained witho4 destroying British credit, and without -danger ef insolvency and distress hereafter, We must save. Every penny spent in extravagance is an aid to our enemies. Every penny saved will help eur country's cause. A great campaign has been opened through- out the country urging the necessity for thrift Meetings will be held in Warrington in due course, and literature distributed With the object of explaining more fully the need and import- Ce of economy. We earnestly hope that the national 'appeal for thrift will arouse a lively interest in the town and will receive a ready and loyal respense from the people Of Werrington. (Signed) W. PETER RYLANDS, Chairmdn. CHARLES E. PARKER, Vice-Chairman. WILPRID J. ROWLAND, Hon. Sec.

NATIONAL REGISTER.

Local Compilation Arrange-

merits Well In Hand.

All the arrangements for taking the local edition of the National Register are well in hand.

A large clerical staff, mostly composed of ladies, has been engaged in. the Bonough Gymnasium this week, filling up the enumera- tion books, which have now been completed. In fact, they were handed to the enumerators last night. The actual delivery of the papers to the various houses, &c., in the town, will commence on Monday, and will be continued throughout the week. The following Monday the work of collecting and collating them will commence. Persons concerned are asked to facilitate the work of the honorary staff by supplying the necessary particulars aceurately, and as promptly. as. possible.

In the rural district forms will be left at each house before Sunday next. 1,1134 wtot, ç colAr_se, be correctly filled in.

THE LATE MR. ROBSON.

Secondary School Principal Dies After Long Illness.

A BRILLIANT CAREER.

Warrington scholastic circles are infinitely the poorer by the death of Mr. Ernest S. A. Robson, principal of the Warrington Secondary School and Technical Institute. Mr. Robson hall been in failing health for several months, and he passed away at his residence, Newlands, Walton-road, Lower Walton, at midnight on Saturday. Mr. Robson was 43 years of age, and was a scholar of brilliant parts. He was the third son of Mr. John Robson, of Selbourne Lodge, Sale. As a young man he won a scholarship to Man- chester University from the Manchester Central Higher Grade School, and at the age of 22 he graduated as a Master of Science, three.years earlier—in 1895—having secured his Bachelor's hood and gown. So brilliant were his achieve- ments at the university that the term of his scholarship, which was tenable at Owens Col- lege, was extended. On the arts side, Mr. Rob- son passed the Inter B.A. examination of Lon- don University, and was awarded the Scruton Classical Exhibition, besides being appointed to give the annual lecture before the Inspectors of Training Colleges., The deceased gentleman held with distinction a number of scholastic appointments, the last, of course, being that of principal of the War- rington Secondary School, the present success of which is very largely due to his able direc- tion and enthusiasm. He was a master who earned both the esteem and respect of his con- freres and of his students, and he always strove to make his classes, and later on, the schools over which he presided, as efficient as possible. He was ever enthusiastie in' the cause of scholarship and was delighted to help the promising and willing student along the intri- cate path of study, and nobody was more pleased than he when any of his old pupils -red distinction. Prior to coming to War- rington Mr. Robson had, amongst other appoint- ments, held those of chief assistant master of the Rochdale Organised Science School and lecturer in physics and mathematics at the Royal Technical Institute, Salford. In 1903 he accompanied the Moseley Education Commis- sion to the U.S.A., and he was the author of a useful text book and of• numerous and valuable articles on scientific and educ.ation,al subjects. He took up his Warrington appointment in August, 1904, and eight years ago he was married to Miss L. K. Miller, daughter of the late Mr. Henry Miller, of Safe, who, with two children, is left to mourn him. The funeral took place at Brooklands Ceme- tery, Manchester, on Wednesday afternoon.

Beell:

===== 0002601_19150807_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
AN APPRECIATION.

To have loved great things; to have looked for great things; and to have achieved great things —in the accomplishment or these Mr. Robson has left a noble and worthy memorial of his life.

To the tVarrington Secondary School and Technical Institute he gave of his best; he loved his work, and the school is considerably the poorer by his loss. It is sad that he should be taken away when normally he would have been at the height of his power and usefulness. , He possessed, in an unusually high degree, powers,. essential for the efficient discharge of the duties' of a headmalTter. He was a hard worker, gave ungrudgingly ' of his time to the school, and had the fine personal quality of imbuing 'his staff with like enthusiasm. The school - has been eminently succeesful throughout his headmaster- ship, and the smoothness, of its working is en- tirely due to his special tact and skill as an organiser. There have been many developments, in the organisation and administration of the school during his period of office, and the difficul- ties which necessarily arose only showed his powers to meet them, and to find the best means of 'dealing with them. Mr. Robson had a fine and commanding personality, On unerring judg- ment. of what was possible and what was not. Possessing an iron will, he was immovable in fighting for a principle. In all his undertakings he saw clearly his goat, and in attaining it he followed high ideals, and on no account would he act merely to suit the expediency of the occasion.

Of Mr. Robson's many excellent oharacteris- tics, let there be first a reference to his extra- ordinary memory. To the end of his unfortun- ate and prolonged illness, his mental faculties were remarkably clear, and it was wonderful to hear him speak within a fortnight of his death,

THE REV. S. G. STANTON AND HIS BRIDE.

A. wedding of great interest to Warrington people tool place in the Parish Church on 'Thursday morning, and attracted a particularly large assemblage of well-wishers, both of the contracting parties belonging to well-known and highly-respected families. As a matter of fact, the ehurcll was crowded in every part, a cir- cumstance( which only happens at long inter- vals of years. The bride, Miss Hilda Mary Stansfield, is the only daughter of Mr. William Henry Stansfield, of Sunnymede, Salisbury- street, Warrington, head master of St. James' School, Latchford; whilst the bridegroom is the Rev. Sydney GlNdstene Stanton, son of the late Mr- George Orton etanton, of Liverpool. The bridegroom graduated B.A. at St. John'sl College, Oxford, taking honours in the history school, and • then 'studied at Cuddesdon Theological College, Oxford. He was appointed curate at the Warrington Parish Church at Christmas, 1913, and gime that date has steadily won his way into the affection of the parish- ioners, and has coneolidated his position materially in his sacred calling. He preaches very well indeed, and gives every promise of being a successful clergyman. The bride was educated at Liverpool College, Huyton ; Manchesteillig,h School, and St. Hugh's College, Oxford, where in the same year she took honours in the history school,, exactly the same as her future husband, whom she did not then know; and she would have been entitled to the same degree, but that -women's disabilities at that university prevented the honour from being conferred. Some day, when those sex disabilities have been removed, Mrs. Stanton will be awarded the B.A. she gained as Miss Stansfield. Miss Stansfield, who wain a scholarship to the Manchester High School, and an open seholar- ship to St. Hugh's College, has, for the last three years, been history mistress at Pate's Grammar School, Cheltenham. She is a lady of broad mind, sympathetic disposition, char- acteristics which, combined with her intellec- tual attainments, will make her an ideal leader in parochial life and clergyman's wife.

THE CEREMONY.

The Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) officiated at the ceremony, assisted by the Rev. 11. B. Firth (vicar of St. ,James'), and the Rev. T. Vickers (curate at the Parish Church). The bride, who wee given away by her father, wore a lovely gown of white satin charmouse, trimmed with pearls and Brussels lace. The train was Ornamented with a lover's knot, em- broidered in white silk and pearls, and a spray of orange blossom. She carried a bouquet of lilies of the valley and pink roses. She wore a gold wristlet watch, the gift of the bridegroom, and a cameo brooch. The bridesmaids were Misses Mabel Stanton (sister of the bridegroom), Margaret Riley and Joan Dawson (niece of the bridegroom). Miss Stanton and Miss Riley wore dresses of blue corded silk, with sleeves and vest of ninon, and shoes to match. Their hats were of blue straw, trimmed with dull pink roses and veiled with net. They carried bouquets of pink carnations and wore pearl brooohes, the gift of the bride- groom. -Miss Dawson was attired in a cream lace frock over net,, with pale pink sash and lace cap to match.. She also wore a pearl brooch (the gift of the bridegroom), and carried a fine sheath of carnations.

Master Neville Stanton (nephew of the bride- groom) acted as page. Mrs. Stansfield, mother of the bride, wore a simple but, effective gown, which was greatly admired, of amethyst crepe de chene, and hat to match.

The Rev., J. M. Buckmaster (curate at the Parish Church) fulfilled the duties of best man.

At the conclusion of .the marriage service, which was fully choral, there was a choral cele- bration of the Holy Communion, according to, the Rubric of the Prayer Book. P. M Darby presided at the organ, and as the bride entered, the assembly rose and sang "The Voice that breathed o'er Eden." During the ceremony the hymns "0 perfect love," "Author of love divine," "Love divine, all loves excelling," were also joined in. Mr. Darby's organ selections were: "Bridal March" (Sangster); largo from "New World Symphony" (Dvorak); movements from " Magnifleat " (Lemaign); allegretto in B minor (Guilmant); march from " Lohengrin " (Wagner); " Romance " in D flat (Lemare). As the Rev. and Mrs. Stanton left, Mendelssohn's celebrated "Wedding March" resounded through the building. During the signing of the register Master N. Davies sang "Angels, ever nrignt and fair."

UNUSUAL COMBINATION OF CIRCUM-

STANCES.

The Rector, who proposed the health of the bride and bridegroom, said there was an un- usual combination of circumstances in this wed- ding, in which they saw a clergyman being married at the church in which he officiated to a lady who was returning to her own native place. lb bad known Miss Stansfield from the. time of her birth, and he had a very high re- gard far her and her parents. lie could not say he had known Mr. Stanton so long, but he had known him long enough to recognise his worth and to think they would be a very happy couple. The Bridegroom responded, and proposed the toast of the bridesmaids. for whom the Rev. J. M. Buckmaster responded. The latter also read a large number of telegrams which had been re- ceived during breakfast congratulating the happy couple. Mr. Darby, proposing the health of the bride's parents, said it had been a delightful wedding— a pattern wedding by reason of the service in which they had participated. Mr. Stansfield, responding, expressed his pleasure at the way in which the friends and relatives of bride and bridegroom had rallied round them on the joyous occasion. lie had given away a daughter of whop he was not ashamed, a daughter who, he believed, would bring to her husband valuable gifts in parochial life, lie and Mrs. Stansfield had already taken Mr. Stanton to their hearts as a son, and were prepared to act as father and mother to him, in place of those whom he had lost. The speeches were interspersed by songs by Sergt. F. 0. Stansfield (the bride's brother) and Mr. P. Oliver (the bride's cousin).

HONEYMOON IN THE LAKE DISTRICT.

Later in the day the Rev. and Ifit. Stanton set out for the Lake District, where they are spending the honeymoon. The bride's dainty travelling dross consisted of powder blue coat and skirt, with embroidered blue satin collar and cuffs; black hat veiled in net, with net crown, trimmed with velvet and pink roses.

THE LATE AIR. ROBSON.

when he was, physically, as helpless as an infant, of the old boys who were fighting for their country. He knew all their little peculi- arities which distinguished them from their fellow pupils—their escapades, their school suc- cesses, their doings on the athletic field—pupils who had 'been left nine or ten years, he remem- bered them all:

Mr. Robson was an omnivorous reader. Read- ing was latterly his only pastime, and ma-ny and various were the books he read. Re would read French and German books, wrestle with Latin prose and verse, and on all he would pass those sharp, incisive comments, which showed at once thet extraordinary versatility of his mind. -,

Above all, he had great influence as a teacher. Testimonies from his old pupils suffice to show this. One of his great joys was to see the old pupils at the annual reunions, and at these gatherings there were many expressions of great appreciation of his teaching. In his teadhing he was never dull, never commonplace; there was a simple directness, coupled with great driving power, which impelled his pupils on- wards and made their progress rapid and sure. To some, Mr. Robson would appear to be harsh, but this was superficial only. At the bottom he was a distinctly generous and affec- tionate man. His feelings at times were quick and impulsive, and there would occasionally be momentary torrents of anger and- indignation, but his forgiving nature very quickly put his pupils at their ease. His character abounded with straightness and courage. He courted no sympathy throughout his long illness; of mock sentiment he had none; he pursued an even, narrow course, which, because go few travel in it, gave him a distinct individuality. As his old pupils look back into their schooldays and recall their memories of him, they will recollect the "fine spirit in which his work was done, the generous motives which were seen to actuate him, and the high degree in which he was known to love that knowledge of which for the time he was lA their eyes the chief representative."

LATCHFORD NUPTIALS.

Well-known Local Singer Wedded At Wesleyan Church.

The Latchford Wesleyan Church was, cm' Monday afternoon, the scene of a pretty wed- ding, the contracting parties being Miss Violet Marion Brown, the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. G. C. Brown, of Lock Villas, Latchford Locks, Warrington, and Mr. Fred Brooke, the only son of Councillor and Mrs. Jas. Brooks, of Whitwell House, Leigh, Lancs. The bride is very well known in local musical circles as a vocalist of outstanding merit, and for the last three years she has been engaged on the variety stage, having toured the Macnaghten circuit. She has not, however, made a pro- fessional appearance at the local Macnaghten house—the Hippodrome—but she has srung on the stage of the Royal Court, taking part in a benefit accorded to Mr. A. M. Loader, the popular general manager of Sir Thos. 13eecham's theatrical enterprises. Miss Brown was professionally known as Miss Inglebury Neilson. - - -

A large number of friends attended the wed- ding service, which was performed by the Rev. A. J. Morton, and was fully choral, the hymns " The voice that breathed o'er Eden" and "0, perfect love" being sung, whilst the organist (Miss Hartley) played the "Bridal March" from "Lohengrin" as the wedding party assembled, and Mendelssohn's beautiful wed- ding march from the "Midsummer Night's Dream" music as the newly-wed couple left the church.

RECHABITES AT BRADF;OIID

Big Increase In Membership Of

Order.

In connection with the forty-aeveatk Iltigh Movable Conference of the Independent Order e? Rechabites, which was held ia Bradford this week, some 8,500 persons, largely mad. up of the members of the juvenile tents, on Sixtganlay marched from the oentre of Um ()Ay to -nook House, the officers of the various tents and dis- tricts wearing the regalia of the Order, and the children being in many cases attired in -fancy costumes, and accompanied by famous Lain Is. , Prizes had been offered.

The Warrington branch ef the Order was represented at the Conference. The biennial report stated that out ef the 319,754 adult members of the Order, over 43,008 are serving with the colours, and two Rechabitea have won the Victoria Cross. That the -loons bers remaining at home fully recognise the patriotism of their comrades is shown by the, fact that a scheme has been in operation or some time whePeby the contributions of those on active service are being paid for them. The King's pledge to abstain from intoxicating liquors and to discontinue their use in the .Royal. households, is hailed with satisfaction as giving the temperance movement an enhanced im- portance, the effect of which will remain. for long years after the war. Regret is expressed that the late Government's 'liquor restrictions were not made more drastic,.

Considering the adverse motion the war haw had on all societies depending to any extent upon propaganda work for keeping up their strength, the membership figures are stated to. be satisfactory. Tbe total membership Is re- turned at 645,994, the increase during the two, years being 38,138. This is only slightly warse than the figures presented at Southport four years ago; the figures of two years ago, owing to the Insurance Act, being hardly a reliable basis for comparison.

THE KAISER'S WILL.

A nroud, cocksure "forked radish" am .1; My right to reign who dare deny? Two arms have I, like the rest of the race, And a nose and a mouth, land a passable

• "face"; But I happen to be the eldest son 01 a poor "forked radish" whose day is _done, And he happened to sit on a oertain throne— He died of cancer, I'm bound to own, And even I, like the rest, shall di*: The worms are waiting patiently— But what does it matter? I claim to rule, And f" the World is on, enormous Fool." It shall march, I swear, to my German drill: "Woe to those who resist my will!"

How dare men breathe the common air With "God's ticegerent" standing there? There is little about me to back the claim, But It boldly make it all the same, And I seek to prove it with fire and sword; I batter churches and drench the sward With the blood of women and children sweet— Such as gathered at Jesu'e feet,— Let rape run riot—A king am With murderous Zeppelins cloud the sky; Stab great ships silently unaware, And with deadly poisons pollute the air. God works through his chosen agents still: "Woe to those who resist my will

I will ride rough-shod o'er a cringing world; My blood-stained standards shall not be furled .Till the haughtiest nations my pity entreat And the humblest lick my conquering feet. -That my Gprman "Kultur" they all may learn, Their towns I shatter, their books I burn. I am fighting that all men may be free To bow their trembling knees to me. No law shall stand in my ruthless way; God smiles on ins the more I slay, And what is "God's vicegerent" for If not to deluge His world in war; To shoot and plunder and lie and kill? ",Woe to those who resist my will 1"

In the heights up yonder, unseen and alone, God sat on His, eternal throne. The planets moved on their shining way; He watched the changes of night and day; He looked on his poor world bathed with tears, And a movement passed through the shining

• spears Of the sons of morning in every star, And His flaming seraphs made ready for war. They stood unseen by our fighting men Till each man felt the strength of ten; And, at last, the dim blue skies were riven, And out of the awful silence of heaven I heard 04:xl's voice through the universe

thrill: "Woe to those who rist My Will!"

ARTHUR BENNETT.

STOCKTON HEATH GARDEN SOCIETY

Exhibition Reflected " Very Greatest Credit."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESIDENT SAYS: "KEEP GOOD INSTITUTIONS GOING,"

It vras the irony of fate that the rain which had refused to fall earlier in the season, when it was badly needed, should come in at the death, or, in ether words, descend in atich quan- tities on show day as to considerably affect the _attendance at the annual exhibition of the Stockton Heath. Garden Society. Approval of the action of the Society, in.-?deeiding to hold the show this year despite the War, was - general, and this sympathy, with the Society's decision was reflected in the attendance, which, considering the rain, was very gratifying. There were 'many lovely exhibits, and the all-round quality gave much ,satisfaction to the ljudges. As for the entries, the number, in the aggre- iate, was down, but the total of competitors was higher than last year.

AN EARLY BAPTISM.

The show; which was the Society's fourteenth

an-na4 exhibition, was held in Wijderspeol Park (kindly loaned by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., Ltd.) Rain commenced to .fall at two o'clock, and, .as it continued for nearly three-quarters of an hour, the opening .ceremony, which was to have been performed by Mrs. Frank Cooper, of Hill Cliff°, did not take place till about a quarter to three, when "it was announced that Mrs. J. Charlton Parr, of Grappenhall Heys, was acting in the stead ,of Mrs. Cooper, the latter lady being indis- posed. A number of; wounded soldiers from various local hospitals, accepted, the committee's invitation to be present at the show, Lind, as they entered the yard adjoining the Park, 'they came in for a hearty cheer.

them up in times like the present? (Hear, hear.)

Congratulations on the success of the show are due to Mr. E. G. White, the keen and capable hon. secretary; Mr. J. Holt, who has rendered invaluable help as treasurer; and the following committee; Messrs. W. H. Woodcock (chairman), J. H. Fletcher (vice-chairman), F. Baker, J. B. Baxter, J. Bennett, D. S. Birrell, J. :Blinston,- J. Bretherton, F. "Broady, J. D., Cannel!, J. Colley, J. Ellison, J. F. Ferguson, W. Hankay, H. B. Houghton, G. Houghton, W. Howard;: J. McDermid; R. Mochrie, J. Morris, J. .Owen, F. Rathbone, J. Richardson, W. Skelton, F. Timperley, W. A. Timperley and E. Vernon.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSIC AND MMTH.

During the afternoon the Warrington 1t.A.0.8. Military Band discoursed admirably, and excellent entertainments were given by Mr. W. W. Whittle's Costume Concert Party. The quality of the party's contributions was un- deniable, and the audiences were very appreciative. Miss Elsie Hewitt sang delight- fully, end Messrs. J. Sephton (tenor), Crowe (alto), Dutton (baritone) and Hopkins (bass) each helped to make up most enjoyable entertainments. Of Mr. Whittle perhaps the best thing we can say of him was that he was his own mirth-breeding self. In the evening sports were held. Teas were provided by Mrs. fitkinaon, of the Bridge Cafe, and a refresh- ment bar was in charge of Mr. W. Mclntyre, and genial host_ of' theßed Lion Hotel.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHOW.

Altogether there were well over five hundred exhibits. Amongst the flowers, the sweet peas led the way. The competition was extremely keen, and the general standard was more than ordinarily creditable. Taking the weather into consideration, the 'roses too, were highly com- mendable. Vegetable's were very good, and. the Game may be said of the fruit, though there was little shown owing to the weather that

has prevailed. The'remark of one of the judges that: "The quality of tne stuff reflects the very greatest credit on the district; taking the .show all round, it's really splendid," should- be encouraging to the Society in the fine work it is doing. There was a Smaller number of entries in the outside exhibits section, but the rivalry was as brisk as over. The juniors gave a. good account of themselves with some pretty collections of wild flowers and grasses. Eggs and butter and dressed- poultry were well to the fore. The exhibits in the gift class, which was open to everyone, were sold by Mr. H. B. Houghton for the benefit of local war funds. One of the prettiest and,most tasteful exhibits in the show was that vatich won first prize in the table decoration section. In this class the destinations of the prizes were decided by popu- lar vote, and it was a most artistic display of harebells that came out at the head of the poll. There were several fine non-competitive and trade exhibits. Messrs. Bees, Ltd. (of -Chester) put in a beautiful show of roses 'and herbaceous blooms; Mrs. J. C. Parr, of Gra.ppenhall Heys had another exceedingly attractive and varied array; and Mr. H. R. Armitage, of Walton Lea, contributed an excellent collection of flowers and plants.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPENING.

Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., C.V.O. (president of the Society), took the chair for the opening ceremony. He was accompanifd by Lady °meanll.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
"SU NNI eR SKIES."

The President said they all wished very much that the show had been held under sunnier skies, not only with regard to the weather,‘ but to the twrible war which was causing so much depression. He hoped that before he took the chair again they would have finished with the war and be at peace with the whole world. They were pleased to welcome amongst them some of the wounded soldiers. They were always glad to do anything they could for those brave men who fought their battles and they appreciated the great work the soldiers were doing in the ranks of the fine Army which had been put in the field. They also appreciated the tremendous amount of silent work the, great British Navy was doing, .enabling us to live in 4appy security. He believed the visitors would agree that the Society was justified in holding the show when they saw the exhibits. It was a big task to organise such an exhibition in these awkward times. There were over 500 very fine. exhibits, which he was sure they would appreciate. Ile was very sorry Mrs. Frank Cooper was unable to attend, and they wished her a:speedy return to health. They had with them, however, their old friend, Mrs. Parr, who had kindly conse*cd to open the show. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19150807_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIERS' LOVE OF FLOWERS.

Mrs. Parr, having voiced her _regret at the absence of Mrs. Cooper and expressed the hope that she would have an'early recovery, said She was always glad to help her friends and neigh- bours, and she liked to feellhat she had a great anany of both .in , Stockton Heath: They all knew what a source of pleasure, flowers were to our wounded soldiers and sailors. She had great Pleasure in declaring the show Open. (Applause.) Miss: Vera White (the little daughter of the lion Secretary) presented Mrs. Parr With a fine bouquet. --- The Rev. J. Collins (vicar) proposed a vote - of thanks ,to Mrs. Parr, remarking that she was always ' interested in what went on in -.Stockton Heath. • -

The' proposition was seconded by. Mr. Thomas Baxter and heartily 'carried. "IN THE DARK DAYS."

W: H. Woodcock (chairman of the com- mittee) in proposing a vote of thanks to Sir Gilbert Greenall, said he had been a very loyal, Idithful and continuous helper of the Society.' He became- President in what they might call the 'rather ,dark days of the Society, when they were labouring under an adverse balance at the bank. Sir Gilbert then made the curious premise that he would remain President so long as the adverse balance remained. This, however, had, the effect of Bfitcrulatintilient to' further efforts, and by the end ,of the third year they got The balance very substantially on the right side. It remained on the right side up "to the present, andthey still had Sir Gilbert as president. The Garden Society was really doing a wonderfully good work in the district, but at a time like this, when we were living under the cloud of' war, it required some little courage to run the society. The committee felt, however, that as it was urged that villagers should cultivate produce in their, own gardens,, it was right thatthe show should be held. They decided to invite their wounded soldier friends to come and see something of the beautiful things that were grown. It was astonishing to see how the wounded appreciated flowers. (Ap- plau.se.)

Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins seconded and wished the Society every success.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
"MORE NECESSARY THAN EVER."

The President, replying, said it was due to tho work of Mr. White, Mr. Woodcock, and the energetic members of the committee that the Society was in a sound financial position. The work the committee had done in the last fow, years had been enormous. If such events es that day's were good for the country in peace 11=5, Low mucb, more -necessary was it to keep

STOCKTON HEATH.

• Prize Day At Stockton Ledge School.—No invitations to parents and friends having been issued, and the usual garden party, and exhibi- tion having been abandoned, owing to the war, the proceedings at the annual prize day at the :Stockton Lodge School were this year very con- siderably curtailed. The successful pupils received their prizes at the hands of Mr. A. C. Lerma, of Myddleton House, while the Prin- cipal, Miss Partingtou, read a long list of suc- cesses, fifty-seven certificates having been gained in various examinations since the last prize day. The prizes having been distributed, a short but excellent programme of vocal and instrumental mUsic was given by the pupils.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
PAROCHIAL GAS BILL.

Committee Want Further Details Before Paying.

The Parochial Committee met on Tuesday evening in the Council Schools, when there was but little business to transact. Mr. G. Piling was in the chair and there were also in attendance : Messrs. H. J. Westbrook, F. Pearson, J. Hallows, F. Warburton. and J. McDermid.; 'with. Mr. W. E. Hough• (deputy clerk), Mr. S. Wright_ (surveyor) and Mr. E. Bradshaw (clerk to the Parish Council). • Credits Allowed. For Unlighted 'Lamps.

The Deputy Clerk read a letter from the Warrington Corporation Gas Department in response to the committee's request for further details in connection with their ,street-lighting account from December to June. The account, it was explained, allowed credits for the periods in which certain lamps were unlighted in con- sequence of the military order. demanding reduced public lighting in the locality. Mr. Westbrook said that the Gas Department had given "only the method of calculation of •the charges and not the details." - The Deputy Clerk: It is a bit of a puzzle at first! We could not possibly work it out unless we had their figures before us. Mr. Westbrook- proposed, and it was decided, ask the Gas. Department for details as to the nOnber of 'lamps' not lighted; the number Of hours they would have. ,been lighted under iormal- conditions and the charge :for con- sumption of gas per lamp. • . , A Tree in the Way.

:Alluding to the complaints made at a pre- vious meeting as to the illegibility of the sign- post in Nictoria-square; Mr.. Hallows said it 'vs;as the tree which blocked the view of the post. - The Chairman said there would be an altera- tion when the shelter for the car terminus was built.

A King's Great Modesty. Once the late King Oscar of Sweden was 'in- soecting a class of young girls, who were natur- ally overcome by a visit from their sovereign. "Can you tell me," asked King Oscar," the names of the great Kings of Sweden?" - "Gustavus Adolphus," answered one girl. "Charles X 11.," responded another. "Oscar II.," stammered the smallest, who was something of a courtier in her way. The King, much amused, went up to her and asked her to tell him of the great events of Ills reign. The child blushed, hesitated, and finally, in tears, murmured "I don't know any." The King smilingly stroked the child's hair and said : "Dolet. cry, dear. I don't know any ray-'

LAY MM

Private Peter Percival—Private Peter Perci- val, of the Ist Cheshires—who was -wounded in the thigh by shrapnel several weeks ago—writes to his wife at 3, West Hyde: "I have been out three days sitting in a chair. I have not learnt to walk yet. They put all the beds outside from my ward, but the weather is so bad that it makes it rather awkward. When a shower comes there is a rush to get us in again. I am going on fine at present, and if I keep it up I shall soon be marked for convalescent again."

Lymm Chemist's Distinction.—The results have just been published of the June exaMina- tion for the Fairchild Scholarship and. prizes in pharmacy. In these the prize for England (...t5) has, we are pleased to say, been. carried off by Mr. William Rigby, of Westhoughton, neai Bolton, who was second in order of Merit for the whole British Isles. Mr. Rigby. is. well known in Lymm, as he acts 4s assistant to Mr. F. E. Evans, chemist, of The Cross. It is only a shirt time ago since he gained the Manchester Pharmaceutical Association's prize for all sub- jeCts connected with pharmacy.

NATIONAL REGISTRATION.

VOLUNTEERS' "HOLIDAY."

Strenuous Work Instead Of Chester

Inspection.

The Lyram platoon of the. Cheshire Volun- teers had a busy day on Bank Holiday Mon- day. At a quarter to ten in the morning they fell in at the Drill Hall and marched to Buck- low Ilill—where they met the remainder of the battalion, who had marched from Altrincham. All then proceeded along the high road to Mere,. coming to a halt in the New Park ad- joining Mere Hall. _ _ _

For half an hour they then proceeded imme- diately to do battalion drill, Major Milne and the adjutant commanding. _ _

By i;ow it was one o'clock, and the men were dismissed for two hours, partaking of the din- ners which they had taken with them. Mineral waters were provided on the field. So far the weather had left nothing to be desired. During dinner several heavy showers came on, and a little thunder was heard. Afterwards the Lymm men found much to amuse them.

At three o'clock they again fell in and pro- ceeded with drill for two and a .half hours, then dismissing for ten minutes in order to prepare for the home march. They arrived home at eight o'clock. We learn from C those in command that the drills were excellently performed, and are ad- judged a great improvement upon the already good 'form of the platoon.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
INK HOLIDAY IN LYIIM.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Would you kindly allow me a short space in your valuable paper on the above sub- ject? Being at liberty for:a few hours on•gonday I decided to have a walk to tha village. I had not been in long when I saw the most disgrace- ful sights it has been my lot to witness for many years. I refer to a large number of Women trippers. Now, Sir, I believe in all men, women and children enjoying themselves to their utmost content and happiness; in a right and legitimate manner. But what hap- pened on Monday? Nine out of ten women were much the worse for intoxicating drink. A number were not capable of looking after them.. selves, while -some, were shockingly indeeent, many. respectable .Lymm people .being .'ashamed to pass through their own village. What shall we say when it comes to a woman turning somersaults?

I consider that Monday's disgraceful scenes will be 'a great lesson to the young men and women of Lymm who saw these women under the influence of drink and acting in such an unseemly and indecent manner. You will notice I do not say anything of the men. There were very few men with the women, and they were less inebriated than the females.

Some weeks ago Councillor Cowper tackled. our Belgian friends on the question .of drink- ing. Will Councillor Cowper please take this subject of the indecent women up? He would earn my thanks—either him or anyone else— for there were expressions of disgust from many Lymm people, amongst them shopkeepers. ' While our country is passing through this r fearful time it seems horrible that these scenes should occur, when perhaps even these women have husbands and sons giving their life's blood for their (these women's) safety. As I gazed upon the village I looked round to the beautiful scenes of Nature, and I thought of these women who "had eyes and saw not, nor did they understand." And what of the little children whose mothers in some cases were not capable of looking after them? These are to be the followers on, to take the place of this generation. Let our country beware, for what a nation.. sows that shall .it .also Yours, &c., . LY3ilt RESIDENT.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
LYP/IM TERRITORIALS.

MOVED FROM " FINE " VILLAGE TO NEW POSITION.

On Sunday the local sth Cheshire Terri- torials at the Front again went to take part in the , actual fighting—though "farther into France," en route for a place quite different from the last. They wont in good trim, for they had had a "rest," in .a quiet French vil- lage, after their strenuous days in the vicinity of Hill 60. During their, sojourn in this. vil- lage they were splendidly entertained and looked. after.

Many letters have been received ,this week from the "lads," but no news has yet been re- ceived of their participating in more fighting. - Writing early in the week to Miss Bessie Cross, of Bridgewater-street, Private Percy Clare said: "We are still well away from the noise of the battle. We are billeted in a very nice schoolroom in a little French village. It is fine. We have been here three days now, and are having a rest before we move on; we are well in France now. We had a grand concert last night—l never saw a better concert in Lymm. We have a stage and everything com- plete, just like the Drill Hall stage, only not as large, and we are having a concert to-night. I expect by you receive this we shall be on the move again; I think we are going by train, as we have a long way to go." At the same time a card written previously on Tuesday, July 28th, ran: "We are out of the trenches."

On Wednesday night a letter dated. Friday, said: "The boys are in the pink. We are still in the French village, but are expecting to move on Sunday, the first of August. My word, time has soon passed. It will soon be twelve months since we were called up, and we shall shortly have had six months out here.. I shall soon have another birthday out here, but I hope I don't see my next here, I •think we shall be at home for that, with a bit of luck." To his mother, Mrs. G. Davies, of Booth's Hill, the Private wrote from the 'village: "Henry Brookes arrived all right yesterday afternoon [Friday, after a few days' leave at home]. . . . It is fine to be in this little vil- lage, out of the noise of the guns and shells. We can hear nothing of that here; we are miles away from the trenches. Don't get down- hearted. We are all in the pink, and looking forward to the time when we shall be welcomed back to our homes by our loved ones; and I don't think that is long off now.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
"Saw Robert Struthers."

"We shall be sorry to leave this village, as everything is so nice and quiet. We cannot tell that there is a war on. . . . We saw Robert Struthers yesterday driving a motor van—you know, brother to A. Struthers, from Welford's." Corporal Jack H. Moston writes: "I am keeping well and going on all right."

Back With Boys Again.

On Wednesday Mrs. J. Taylor, of 1, West Hyde, received from her son, Private Henry Brookes, word as follows : "I arrived safe and am keeping quite well. I found our regiment resting in this peaceful little village, a good number of miles behind the firing line; I think we shall be some time before we go in the trenches again. I felt. hurt at having to leave y(211 all, as I quite enjoyed my short stay. When I got back here there was a letter from my [step] father [also a soldier], and he was saying how nice it would be if we could all be at home together. But perhaps the next time I come he will be able to get. . . . We have seen Roland Dixon and Robert Struthers. and I believe Jock Gibbons [of Newfields] was through, here the other day. .

===== 0002601_19150807_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
Private Henry Cadman.

Private Henry Cadman, of the Tunnelling Section, writes to his sister, Mrs. S. Owen, Brookfield Cottages: "I was very sorry to hear about you at Lymm thinking that I was blown up. Henry Brookes told me, when he got back, about it. I should have written before now, only we have been moving to another place, and we have not got there yet. I am with our boys again until we land where we are going, and I am glad to say that they are all looking fine. I am not feeling badly, only I have not much colour now, as I had before I went on the tunnelling work. But it is with not being used to it, and will all come to again shortly."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
INDIVIDUAL CASES.

Private Jim Taylor, of 1, West Hyde, arrived back safely at the Lincoln County Hospital after four days' visit home. Unfortunately he is not very well at present, • having caught a chill.

Private William Percival, of Moston-street— who was wounded a few weeks ago in the 'right leg—paid a visit home this week to his parents. Coming from the Banstead Military Hospital, Surrey, he arrived late on Friday evening, re- turning yesterday to the other " Teviers" in Shropshire. His leg is not yet quite better, and will not be so for some time.

Private James Leah, of The Rookery, crossed on Sunday from hospital at Le Treport, France, to the Ist London General Hospital, St. Gabriel's College, Camberwell. Writing from there to his wife, he says: "It leaves me very near all right again, hoping it will not he long before I see you all again. I. got in London yesterday (Sunday) morning at ten o'clock, and only wish they had sent me a bit nearer than this. It is a pleasure to be away from the sound of the guns and rifle shot; it is quite a different sound altogether to hear the old street organ again—quite a treat. I have not got out of bed yet, and all they are feeding me on is milk... I feel I can eat a horse. . . I have not had a bit of bread for a month now; it has been all milk.- I am very near dropping for something." On Thursday afternoon Private Stanley Gibbs of henry-street, came over from Toxteth Park Military Hospital, Liverpool, returning in the evening. He has not by any means recovered the use of his wounded hand, but is making good. progress.. . _ _

- Priv-ate- Gibbs has had two bones extracted from his hand, but he is to go under another operation—when sinews will be transplanted into his hand. He was at first to have come over on Wednesday, but was unexpectedly obliged to postpone his visit home for a day.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOHN THOMASON.

Explains Progress And Dangers Of His

Wound.

Mrs., J. Thomason, .of - Lymm Bridge,: New- road, received on Monday the following letter from .her husband; lying in the Sheffield Military Hospital "I have. had My X-ray report, and have seen for myself that there is a piece of loose bone in the joint of my ankle. Well, that piece of bone is what has caused all my previous aches and pains, and it has got to stop in—it has been in too long to be meddled with now. I have only got to wait for the wounds to heal up, and then I shall be home for a week."

On Wednesday morning Private Thomason wrote: "You will be glad to know that one wound is completely healed up—the one where the shrapnel was extracted. I have • been out for one hour this afternoon, but of course I cannot expect to get up and walk right away. You see I don't know whether I shall get the use of the sinew at the back right again; I shall have to wait and see, and hope for the best. Of course when Ido get better it will not be any better than it was before, because of the loose piece of bone which is still in the joint. It is one year to-night since we mobilised, but I did not think then that we should really have to go—in fact, I did not until Christmas time. Well, let us 'hope it is over as sudden as it started."

Lance-Corporal Hillier.—Lance-Corporal Reg. Hillier, of the Manchesters—who was wounded several weeks ago during a fierce engagement at the Dardanelles—writes to his parents, Mr. and Mrs. W. E. Hillier, of the Village, to say he is progressing well, though necessarily slowly, and that he is expecting to be moved to a con- valescent hospital.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MEDITERRANEAN.

IMPRESSIONS OF THE ARMIES FIGHTING TO VICTORY.

Sergeant Thomas W. Field, of the 1/7th Battalion Lancashire Fusiliers, Mediterranean Expeditionary Force, has written us the following significant letter, which he dates July 24th. The lucidity of thought displayed in the letter (which was written in the trenches under fire) demonstrates how effectively the members of our expeditionary forces can train themselves to withstand at least a few of the many terrors to which they are exposed. Sergeant Field, who is one of our valued correspondents, lives in peace times at New- fields, Lymm.

"I cannot (as probably you are aware) say what 1 would like to you for, not being an authorised correspondent with the forces, my letters go through a channellikely at any time to be picked up by our enemy; which, if con- taining experiences .and figures, would be of material interest to them, and .I should as a soldier feel ashamed of -my personal impatience and folly. -Yet 'I can; I think, by now tell you that we are opposed strenuously against the wily Turk and his German 'pal '—who, by the way, if information received be correct, are likely to quarrel at any time. So if rogues fall out honest people receive their own; and should they not fall out we are likely to get more than our own in a decided victory on this peninsqla 'ere long. So it matters not much either way, only they will, by falling out, save a good many lives.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ADMIRED TURK.

"The Turk as a soldier has so far received a. Certain amount of admiration from our men for his fighting qualities, especially in trench- warfare; and I do honestly believe that were it not for either his misuse or misunderstand- ing of the divine rights of use of the white flag we should not complain at all. -Up to the time of writing we hal>e had no official news of his endeavouring to poison the water supply to the extent that . . . The health and immunity from fever of our ,men are good. Of course, we take .every precaution, and we have a good supply of qualified people with us who test the stuff we drink often.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
"GERMANS WRONG AT ANY TIME."

"We do not mind the Turk so much, who fights honestly. But we do mind his 'pal,' who is likely to go wrong at any time; so we are alert. You would laugh at a printed circular that I have. It has been dropped from one of their air machines, telling us it will be better for us to surrender, and they will treat us well. We have had some fun with it. We show no preference to either German or Turk, as we shoot them both; yet we think more of the Turk. WHAT SURPRISED EVERYBODY.

"The quantity of gunboats arid transports and soldiers has not only surprised the Turks but our men as well, who have a few of those people called Terriers' among them—of whose value as ;;oldiers I shall have something to say in the near future, should I not perish in this great struggle for independence and freedom. "The only defect I have suffered up to now has been sun glare; I am pleased to say both my eyes are 'back to normal' now. It's rather difficult to aim correctly when you cannot see, so I am thankful for recovery. I am pleased to say' I have not been away from my company since we left old England. a single day; so I have some material and items of interest to write about when the opportunity occurs.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
ARMY KNOWS NO POLITICS.

"I was sincerely sorry to see in one of the issues of the 'Warrington Examiner' the photo and news of Walter Jackson, of Lymm, perish- ing; and I trust God will be generous to his people in the future, for there is nothing so terrible to any household as the loss of its chief. I see items of interest in The Examiner only at times, as our mails are not so regular and things find us in such queer places you would really be surprised. _ _ _ _

"We have heard so much about slackers in England that the universal vote here is: Con- scription t I offer no opinion as yet, for the Army knows no politics, neither does it recog- nise politics. (This desk of mine is not so good —my knee—so please excuse scribble!) When it is thoroughly known at home of the splendid work done here by 'Terriers' you will be astonished. I have been intimate with the qualities of ' Terriers ' and Volunteers since 1898, but it has opened nay eyes, to say the least of it, and I am only too sorry I cannot now give you some material that would hardly look believeable.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM RED CROSS PRAISED.

"I received a photo of the Lymm Red Cross Hospital, and it looks fine and creditable. I am rather proud of it. I am also pleased to tell you I have been promoted from Section Com- mander to Platoon Sergeant. I will at the very first opportunity give you something in- teresting—unbridled and truthful—of our doings, but, as yet our safety and ultimate suc- cess depend on personal patience and fortitude."

KICKED EN FACE WMI SPURS.

Drunken Soldier's Brutal Act At Lynun.

At the Lynim Police Court on Wednesday, before the Iron. J. E. Cross (in the chair) and Vice-Chaneellor_D. Stewart Smith.

The remanded case of Joseph Leys, of 109. Sanderson-street, Miles Platting, Manchester—a private in the 10th Hussars, at present on leave from the British Expeditionary Force at the Front—was brought up. Leys was charged with unlawfully wounding Charles Harry Skidmore, of 5, Booth-street, Warrington, on August 2nd, at Lymm. Skidmore is a inrnaceman in the employ of Messrs. Monks, Hall, Warrington. The charge was reduced to one of common. assault, to which prisoner pleaded guilty. ' Unjustified Disturbance.

William Travis, licensee' Oi the Varmer'b Arms, Heatley, stated that Leys came to his house at about 4 p.m. on August Monday, but he noticed the man had already had enough to drink, and, telling him he could not supply him iVith more, requested liim:to leavethe,premises. Leys at once bcgan to use Very abusive language and refused to leave. He was therefore put out through the front door, but he immediately rushed back and got behind the bar, trying to get at witness. Mrs. Travis got between them, and (wae.. struck-. by the in;iiOlier, Who was still using very 'vile language. A customer named, Skidmore, who was at the time in the tap room, went to witness's assistance, and the man was got out through the door a second time. The door was next locked. In the struggle Skidmore had also been pushed out with the other man, and later witUess saw him with his face covered with blood and, taking him.ln, bathed his. face, and advised him to go to the doctor.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
Shut Out Accidentally.

Mailes Hairy Skidiaors said that he was in the tap-room when he heard a disturbance. Leys was making use of very bad language. Seeing that the licensee and his wife were in difficul- ties,he went to their assistance. In helping to get the disturber outside he himself got pushed out in the struggle, and two or three men who were with Leys hustled witness, who was knocked down. Leys, who was wearing spurs, kicked him in the face. The men then went away. When witness was able to get up he found that his face was covered with blood, issuing from two severe wounds on his lip. He went to Dr. Thorpe, at Heatley, who put in three stitches.

Mrs. Alice Skidmore, wife of the last witness, deposed that she was at the same time in the Farmer's Arms with her husband, and corrobor- ated everything. Alter the scuffle in the house she found her husband had got shut out, and looking through the window saw him lying on the ground and Leys kicking him. Prisoner's Original Contention.

Sergeant Davies, of the Lymm police, stated that at about 4.30 p.m. on Monday Skidmore came to him bleeding from two wounds on the upper lip. In consequence of what he was told, the Sergeant apprehended prisoner at Warburton, close to The Saracen's Head, half. an hour later—telling him that he was to be charged with unlawfully wounding Skidmore. He then brought him to the Police Station. Ley-s said he had- not wounded ariybody; -a -woman .had knocked him down in the Farmers' Arms. At

Tuesday's Court and previously he made no reply to the charge: I' Prisoner, who called n& witnesses, said 111 was very sorry. He had left Manchester on that afternoon in a wagonette with a number of former workmates, and with him being back from the Front they gave him a lot of drink on the way, thinking they were doing him a good turn.

"Leniantly Dealt With."

Prisoner was fined forty shillings and <el 15s. special costs. The Chairman said he must con- sider that he was getting off leniently. Even it men had been to the Front they must behave themselves when they came back. The Chair- man also complimented Skidmore on having came to the assistance of the licensee; he had done very well.

PENKETII.

Accident to a Child.—An unfortunate acci- dent occurred to Roland, a two-year-old grand- son of Mr. and Mrs. Loxley, The Park, Pen- keth, who through some unexplained cause came into contact with the off-side wheel of a contractor's. cart near his home on Saturday last. After a close medical examination it was found that a small bone at the back of one ear was crushed. Happily the patient is slowly recovering from the shock and subsequent ill- effects to one of such immature years.

Special Constables.—The mobilisation of the special constables for this township has taken place, and they are undergoing the necessary drill, under the able supervision of Sergeant Jewett to qualify for professional work should the emergency arise. Thursday night from eight to nine o'clock, is devoted to this in- struction, and the members, who number be- tween fifty and sixty strong, should prove energetic supporters of the peace during any troublous times which it may be our lot to endure in the near future, as well as a source of comfort to our own guardian, Constable Hopson.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
THROUGH YANKEE

GLASSES.

Our American Cousins And Press Criticism.

Our valued American correspondent, Mr. II- Whitham, writes us as follows on what he dubs "a question of policy":— I would like_to make, with the aid of the "Examiner," a suggestion that may have far more point to it than may appear at the first glance. It ought before•now, tohave been clearly understood in the editorial rooms of the British Press, that not one of the strong na- tions of the earth likes to be what I will call, "patronised." The United States is no exception to. this: rule, for an English-born person here in the United States to speak of his love for his Mother Country is all right, and no one takes exception to it, for reasons that are obvious. It is quite another thing when an editorial, appearing in a British journal, at once proceeds to adopt a grand- motherly attitude, and give, to what she is pleased to call, her "kin beyond the sea," good scolding, but couples with it a big dose of most unwelcome advice. Jusk at this time all the leading journals are closely • watched for opinions touching the situation as existing be- tween the Allies and the Germans. Any pointed remarks made by a British journal are at ongs seized hold of by "The Fatherland," Or "TheWssue," and make fine material for the G-erman mobs that gather in the various re- sort&

The following taken from an editorial that appears in my Morning paper under the cap- tion, "Foreign Advice," is plain enough, and to me it realty means, "Give over talking of your "kin beyond the sea" :—"There is no need for American statesmen to turn to London. Paris, or the capital of any other belligerent power for advice in regard to the German situation. Shirt-sleeve diplomacy is well able to take care of all the problems involved, and is not to be intimidated by questions as to whether the United States will endure the re- buff which some consider as being contained in the latest Von Jugow reply."

TRUNKS, DRESS BASKETS, SUIT CASES, Bce. J. S. GARLAND, 51, 53 & 55, BRIDGE STREET, WARRINOTON.

lettas to tbe Onitor

MR. WINFIELD'S_ _STORIES OF

CHURCH LIFE.

Six,—The entertaining storks told by Mr. Mounfield in his articles on old church life have reminded me of a good story which circu- lates still at Kenver in Staffordshire. The ' bobber" did his duty very diligently among the boys and girls and kept them awake with thrusts and raps from his rod. But he allowed the farmers to slumber as long as they wished. This injustice was noticed by the Squire, who said to the clerk, "It is hardly fair to rap the boys only, Thomas. Several farmers sleep regularly. 1 will give you half-a- -trowii if you will rap the eldeDs as well as:the boys!, The following Sunday was very hot and the Squire himself went to sleep. He was awakened by a sharp rap and opening his eyes he saw the clerk standing with his hand open. 'l'll trouble you for that half-crown," he said. 'The Squire paid up without a murmur. On the subject of tongs for extracting dogs from under the church seats, readers who have visited Bangor may remember that a pair of these tongs are preserved in a glass case in the Cathedral. They were formerly in use in the Cathedral, and have spikes which would give a firsn and painful grip upon the dog. INTERESTED READER.

THE "TRUE RELIGION."

Sir,—The sermon of the Rev. Canon Willis, Pl'Wished in .your issue of July 24th, suggests the consideration of a very important question. In dealing with the essentials of Catholicity, the Rev. Canon's explanation of the second essential is that "we must held the Faith Without adding to or taking away from it," Which is very true. But the question which naturally asserts itself is, What Faith? The true one, presumably. But how are we to know it? The Canon did not put forward any method of identifying or recognising it, and so I ven- ture to suggest some considerations. The fact that all religions are contrary to each other demonstrates not only that all are not true, but that only one can be so; and that Abe necessity of possessing the true faith is of such grave ' importance may be , ,judged by Christ's words: "He that beliereth shall be "Salted, but he that believeth not shall be con- demned," Mark xvi. 16. •Believeth what? A very distinct body of doc- trine if the rejection of it merits condemna- tion. But what or whic\h is the true religion? Surely that which presents for our belief God's revelation in its perfection and entirety. Now the truths of revelation, which are Mysteries, are beyond the reach of intellect, and cannot be acquired by speculation (or they would not be mysteries). We can only know them by God manifesting them to us, and He does so by means of the authdbrity which Jesus Christ (God the Son made man) established when on earth. Christ's Words clearly indicate His establishing of an authority to teach: "He that'heareth you, heareth Me," Luke a. 16. "As the Father hath sent Me, I also send you," John xx. 21. "All power is given Me in heaven and on earth: Go therefore and teach: Whatsoever I have commanded you," Matt. xxviii. 18, 19. This then is the manner which God has or- dained by which the "Faith can he known without adding to or taking away"; by re4iv- jug it from the Divine authority, with which is deposited, the Faith. in its perfection and entirety. Private judgment utterly 'frustrates the accomplishing of the Rev. Canon's second essential: A point for earnest consideration. These views may not be shared by all your readers, bilk let him who is in doubt and is desirous of knowing and possessing the true religion, seriously consider that this lutist be the fundamental principle, namely, subeission to authority. • ' JOHN RICHARDSON. 86 ' ,-- iq uttermarket-street, August sth, 1915.
