TWENTY YEARS A CITIZEN SOLDIER.

Death Of A Sergeant Who Joined

National Reserve. The death occurred last week, after an Operation at the Warrington Infirmary, of , • Sergeant T. R. Ashworth, of Goulden-street. Highly respected by all who knew him, the i Sergeant was 50 Tears of age, and was . the Possessor of a long service medal, which he received after some 20 years' association with the old Warrington Volunteers, holding the . rank of Colour-Sergeant when 'he decided to retire. He was employed as a departmental foreman at Messrs. Croi3field's works. When the call came a few months ago for old service men to offer themselves for duty, he again joined the colours with the National Reserve, entering the ranks as a private, but eventually being promoted to sergeant. He was Actively associated with many institutions in the town, and for over 30 years was a loyal member of the Order of the Sons °f Temperance. When the National Service League was first inaugurated, Sergeant Ashworth was found among its most earnest ,supporters, and up to the time of the outbreak of the present war he held an optimistic view of the ultimate success of the movement. He leaves a widow, • two sons and a daughter to mourn their loss. His eldest son is a. member of the 2/4th South Lancs., and is at present in training at Ashford. , A Military Funeral. The funeral, which was of a military .charac- ter, took place on Sunday,, at the Warrington 'Cemetery, amidst many manifestations of deep "egret. The cortege was preceded by a num- ber of the dead Sergeant's fellow workmen, who were joined by Messrs. H. Wardman, G. liend, R. Jones, W. Adams and J. Campaign, representing the Order of the Sons of Tem- Perance. A detachment of the National Reserve followed at the rear of the procession. The coffin, which was draped with the Union Jack, was borne to the grave by a number of Sergeants associated with the National Reserve, and the service in the Cemetery Chapel and at [jilt graveside . was conducted by the ,Rev. Williamson, of Bold-street Wesleyan Church, with which place of warship the deceased had been associated. After the committal service the "Last Post" was sounded by a party of ' buglers. Among the mourners present at the graveside 'r'e. Mrs. Ashworth (wife), Masters T. R. and • Ashworth (sans); Miss A.- Ashworth (daugh- ter), Mr. and Mrs. J. J. Ashworth, Mr. and Mrs. W. Ash.worth, Mr. and Mrs. H. Edwards Piaci Harold Edwards, Mrs. Jordan (mother-in- Law), Messrs. Wm., Walter and F. Jordan, Miss Jordan, Messrs. R. and E. Ashworth, R. Rionmfield, W. Clayton, Billington (represent- -Ing Messrs. Cia-)sfield), Mrs. Thom 'and Mr. and rs• A. Healey. Floral tributes were sent by the •widow and tldren," John, Maggie and. family," "Will, charlotte and family," Mrs: Jordan and family, Mr. and Mrs. •Somers (Stafford), Mr. 'Ball, Mr. 11illington, Quarter-Master-Sergeant (2/4th ~ 'outh Lanes.),, Sergeants 2/4th South Lancs.), Corporals and men (2/4th South Lancs.), Lieut. and Mrs. Wilcocic, "His fellow workmen," and .‘ the neighbours:"

(Eburtij Arius,

A VICAR'S REPROOF.

Writing of public worship in time of war, the Vicar of St. Ann's (the Rev. J. Davies) says: "We have not the joy of saying that many more have joined U 6 in public prayers, or that there has been an increase in the regularity of our members at public worship.. .. . There should be increase in the number of petitioners at the throne of Divine grace—definitely pray- ing for our forces and those of our Allies, for their safety, for their bodily and spiritual health, for victory in the day of battle; praying for a speedy peace, based on love to God and good- will to man—a peace enduring, and one in which the kingdom of God may he strengthened and extended."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCH-GOING IN THE HOLIDAY

MONTH.

Writing in the current ,issue of his Parish Magazine, the Vicar of St: James', Latchford ' (the Rev. H. B. Firth) says:—" August is, as a rule, the favourite month for people to take an annual holiday of longer or shorter duration, and Church attendance in all places except the recognised holiday resorts reaches its low water mark. Whether so many of us will go away this year remains to-be seen. I, for one, intend ' to be absent from the parish for some little time this month., though I do not expect to be away on any of the Simdays. Let us do what we can to secure as, good attendance as possible of worshippers in the Lord's Howie on the Lord's Day. A half-empty church, even in August, is a depressing spectacle: if we .are at home this month let us bear in mind the Apostolic writer's 'advice about 'not forsaking the assembling of ourselves together as the man- ner of some is."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
A NATION'S WORD.

Those :Who Have Fallen And Bled To

Maintain It.

The Rev. H. Ogden preached at Golborne- street -Baptist Church last Sunday, on the eve of his departure to • assist in Y.M.C.A. camp work in the South of England until September 3rd. It was a special intercession service to mark the close of the first year of the war. The reverend gentleman, preaching from 11. Samuel i. 26, "I am distressed for thee, my brother Jonathan," declared that Jonathan was one of the Scripture characters Of whom one might say an "Israelite indeed in whom there was no guile." Amongst dynasts he was a prince ;who conducted no backstairs intrigues. It was to his worldly gain to supplant David, and more so as Jonathan was in the succession. If hereditary claims were pre-eminent the throne of Israel was his. But Jonathan never con- spired against David, except for his friend's safety. David soared, to high eminence; and his fall was all the more terrific. Rumour has never suggested the slightest scandal against The name of Jonathan. lie was a brave prince. ,and as straight and true as he was brave. Well might David exclaim,, "I am distressed for thee, my brother Jonathan." Concerning the 60,000 men in the British Army, they would say, "I am distressed for thee, my brother Jonathan." The prince of Israel . was a victim of war; so are the brave men who have fallen in this conflict Belgium is bleeding. Belgium „may say that Britain guaranteed .Belgian neu- trality and at the critical Moment had no force to fulfil her guarantee. But justly, Britain. can 'say she never anticipated German duplicity, nor foresaw' her Wild- and bestial' cruelty. • But' the 60,000 who: have fallen,' the 331,000 who have suf-. fered and are suffering, have done so to main- tain a nation's word. Britain was safe 'behind her sea wall; but her word was her bond, and • the plains of France and Belgium had buried her noble dead. We join in David's lament. Our deepest sympathy is extended -and our prayers are offered for the dear ones who suffer and who pine and lament in solitude. Rich and poor, high-born and low-born, duchess and peasant woman alike have wounds that the hand of man cannot heal. The Kaiser's Heaviest Crime.

When Lord Roberts was buried _there. was. a

black fringe in the crowd that had special sig- nificance. They were the young widows of the men who had fallen in that retreat from Mons. Lady Roberts had as her companions in sorrow the wives of the almost unknown soldiers. Death is the greatest leveller of human life. We are indebted to the artists who touched cot- tage sorrow with a tender hand. Luke Fildes, in his famous picture, "The Doctor,"faithfully portrays the rude furniture of the cottage with the common domestic accessories, and the poor mother is distracted with grief as she fears the worst *for her bairn. Sorrow grips her heart. There is no sadder picture in war than a widowed mother whose flower has been rudely crushed beneath the tramp of armies. Nothing will console her, and she will droop to her grave. Towns can be rebuilt, but a mother's crushed love can never be restored. No heavier crime lies to the debit of the Kaiser and his minions than the bleeding hearts of women- folk. Human words fail. They never reach deep enough. Mortal hands cannot heal a wounded soul. The best man can say is "That distress almost overwhelms him." Christ can console. He is the real helper. He can console. Never more than now the best friend to have is Jesus. That friend it is the privilege of the church to reveal to sorrow-burdened men and women. The lesson read for the' service was I. Corin-

thians xv. The solo " Lovely peace" was naosi effectively rendered by Mr. Hannan. The subject was "Our fallen heroes."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
CREME OF THE KAISER.

Caused Mankind To Suffer More Than Any King.

At the Working Men's Mission, Bank Quay, on Sunday evening, a special war-intercession service was held. Councillor George Dale gave ! the address, and in the course of his remarks said that the message of the angel to the Church in Smyrna, "Je thou faithful unto death and I will give thee a crown of life," was not only applicable to the individual Christian, but also to the • British people .in .the . present crisis. After twelve months of terrible war, of un- speakable anguish and bloodshed, should they ' despair and say it was •useless to continue the struggle? Sixty thousand brave Britons had given their lives, and 300,000 more had been wounded or taken prisoner. Notwithstanding all that, the work was not yet accomplished. The cry of widows and orPhans- had. risen to heaven and appealed for vengeance—and yet the end seemed far off! They were apt to ask themselves "Should we discontinue the strug- gle," but the answer to such a thought seemed to come in the Biblical message bidding them ,"Be faithful !"

===== 0002601_19150807_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
What Really Matter!

The preacher went on to say, that death came to every man and, whether soon or late, it Mat- tered little. It was whether death found them faithful': which did really matter Were they to sum up the Kaiser's.life as .wert the lives of kings in the days of Old Testament history summed up, they would find that it would read something like this: "The Kaiser reigned so many,.years in Berlin, and built up, by the

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 7, 1915.

aid of his War Lords and mighty men of valour, a great military system such as men had never seen before. And it came to pass, that when the hour had struck, he sent forth a decree, and sought to spread his Empire over the whole of Europe. In so doing he showed no regard for law or international usage. More- over, he supported his advisers when they spoke of the most solemn and honourable treaties as 'scraps of paper.' No Pity and No Reverence.

"And the great and terrible acts that he did in France, in Belgium, and on the high seas, are they not writ in history?—how he showed no pity for women and children, and had no reverence for the temples of God, but caused them to be destroyed and burned with fire, ..But lo! it came to pass that having filled uP the cup of his iniquity, he was humbled to the dust. And so he died and was buried with his fathers, having done that which was evil in the sight of the Lord, and having sinned against God, in that he caused mankind to suffer more sorely than had done any of the kings that had lived before himil" A very different record would, the speaker felt sure, be given in the pages of history con- cerning Albert, King of the Belgians, for it would be written of him that in the day that some men considered the day of his humilia- tion, he stood high, in all the qualities that went to make ilp.lllo. most real dignity and nobility of character.- And the Belgian nation that had been "faithful unto death," • would receive "the crown of life," for after this war there would be a glorious resurrection of a national character that would astound the whole civilistkd world. Faithful Unto Death!

The preacher then referred to the fact that a!, large number of the soldiers engaged in the! present conflict were Sunday school scholars, ! and went on to say that England would always remember the fallen heroes of the great war. It would always be said of them that they were "faithful unto death" in the cause of King and country. In _conclusion, Councillor Dale said that .the best way in which his hearers !could help would be to do whatever -they could to assist the cause of. the Allies during" the remaining period of the war. It was not for him to indicate along what lines they should proceed, for each individual conscience, having duly considered the situation, could-best deter-: mine that. But one thing was. certain; that -every one of them was duty bound to do what- ever might be possible- according to his powers and circumstances not only to make victory certain, but to bring about the desirable climax as soon as possible, or at least as soon as might be consistent with a satisfactory settlement. Suitable hymns were sung during the service.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCH LADS' BRIGADE. Week-end Camp Of The War- rington Cadet Battalion.

By the kindness of Lieutenant-Colonel Lyon, of Appleton Hall, the Warrington Cadet Bat- talion of the C.L.B. was encamped in Appleton Park during last week-end. The officers on duty were: Major J. A. Brookes, commanding; Major C. Ford, acting adjutant; Captain F. Bunch, Quartermaster-Lieutenant N. J. Morris, Sergeant-Major Essenhigh, Sergeant Woods (quartermaster-sergeant), Colour-Sergeant Star- key (sergeant-cook), and Staff-Sergeants Taylor and Rook. Colonel Lyon paid two visits to the camp, and among other visitors were Captain Henshall and Lieutenant Hensliall (4th Senth. Lanca- shires), Lieutenant E. Hall Coates (C.L.8.), and Mr. Beresford Melville._ - - _

The Battalion paraded on Saturday after- noon. under the command of Major J. A. Brookes, and, headed by St. Paul's C. 143. Bugle Band, marched to the camping ground. On Sunday a special communion service was held at Stretton Church at 7.15 a.m., and the Bat- talion also had a church parade at the morning service, the Rev. the Hon. C. F. Cross officiating at both services. ' - -

On Monday the Battalion was inspected by Lieutenant-Colonel Lyon. The inspecting officer was received with a general salute, and after reviewing the march past, he inspected the Cadets, and afterwards made a thorough inspec- tion of the camp kitchens, &c. GOOD LADS MAKE GOOD MEN. In his remarks to the Cadets,

Colonel Lyon said how pleased he was to see them doing their bit, and complimented them upon their work, and especially upon the order I and cleanliness of their camp and equipment. In his-experience a clean soldier was always a good soldier. He also referred to his recent. visit to the firing line in France, and stated that it had brought home .to him more forcibly than anything could do what the advantages and privileges of English people were, and at the same time the importance of training and dis- cipline for all our people. He had, he said, some gOod Warrington lads serving with him, and he exhorted them to stick to their work, so that in their day good men might be found ready for the service of King and country. Hearty cheers were given for Colonel Lyon at the conclusion of the inspection. The afternoon was devoted to sports, and in the evening the camp was struck, the Battalion leaving for home at 8 p.m. St. Elphin's Company were the winners of the prize for the best tent in the lines. •

BOY SCOUTS.

" Crosfield's Own " Troop In Camp at Whitley.

The above Troop held their August Camp at Whitley, and for the week-end were the guests of Mr. and Mrs. Kay, of Morris-hill Cottage. The baggage was conveyed in advance by one of the tirfifs motor lurries. The boys arrived in camp at 3.15 p.m. on Saturday, and by 4.30 p.m. were under. canvas and prepared for a well-earned meal. After tea attacks on the camp were made by Messrs. Kay, Wells and Cobley, only the last named- being successful in making an entry On his second attempt.. . • The orders for the day on Sunday included a route march in the morning, with the band in attendance, Under the command of Instructor B. Wright, and divine service in the evening at the Whitley Chapel, the sermon being de- livered by Mr. Mosse, of Stockton Heath. Sports were held on Monday and an enthusi- astic ' spirit was displayed in each event, the host, who acted as judge, giving special prizes for the half-mile race. The prizes were presented by the hostess to the following boys :- 100 yards flat—Thomas, Martin, Makin. Jockey race.—Sankey and Gibbons, Gleave and Bates. _ _

Half-mile race.—Thomas, Robinson and Col- lins. _ _

Three-legged raee.—Barratt and Whittaker, Winstanity and Appleton. 'Obstacle race,,-Sankey, Robinson and Brown. Egg and spoon race.—Lowe, Jones and Dean. Sack race.—Appleton, Cliffe and Daniels. Potato race.—Houghton, Gleave and Sankey.

Over 70 members of the Troop were present, including Assistant Scoutmasters R. Garner, w. P. Taylor and J. McCawley. After striking camp the Troop was paraded in , front of Morris-hill Cottage, and three cheers accorded the host and hostess., afkan appreciation of the. kind and generous manner in.which-they. had entertained both officers and boys.

IN THE COURTS.

BUTTED THE CONSTABLE.

Two Soldiers Help To Arrest Violent

Man.

Before the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) and other magistrates at the Borough Police Court on Bank Holiday, Patrick Shaughnessy, a farm labourer, of Green Farm, Sankey, was charged with assaulting P.C. Sankey. The officer deposed that he was on duty at the bridge at 10.10 on Saturday night, when he saw prisoner, who was drunk. He requested the man to go borne, but he refused to do so, and witness had to take him into custody. Prisoner became very violent and struck witness three . .

times in the- face and butted him with his head in the chest. Two soldiers, Privates Pember- ton and Daniels, came on the scene and helped to convey the prisoner to the Police Station. Private Pemberton said he saw the prisoner strike the police officer twice; the man was very violent. Witness and a comrade assisted the policeman to get the man to the lock-up.

The Soldiers Thanked.

The Chief Constable said the action of the two soldiers in going to the assistance of the constable was much appreciated by the police. Shaughnessy was sentenced to a month's im- prisonment with hard labour, and the Mayor congratulated Privates Pemberton and Daniels upon their action, and said that the Bench were greatly indebted to them.

AN "IRISH STEW."

Soldier From The Emerald Isle NA ho Wanted To Fight.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Private Thomas Barlow, an Irishman, of the 2nd Battalion of the South Lancashire Regi- ment, was charged with. having been drunk and ,disorderly in.Buttermarket-treet,.On'.Suturclui 'evening; and with haVirig asseUlteir Henry. Hain-. kinson, the landlord of the Crown and Sceptre Inn, Butterrnarket-street- _

, Mr. Hankinson deposed that the prisoner came into his house in a drunken condition and refused to leave when. requested to do so. Wit- ness went to eject him, and when he had got him as far as the door prisoner turned round and struck him a hard blow in the mouth with his fist. He then closed with witness, and get-

ting his arms round witness's neck- bit him through the bottom lip, causing it to bleed con- siderably. Witness went out in search of a policeman, and found P.C. Coombs, who took prisoner into custody. The Chief Constable asked the Bench to re- mand prisoner until Wednesday, in order that Colonel Moggridge migit be communicated with.

Prisoner told the Bench that he had been wounded in Prance more than once, and that he

had been undergoing treatment in an Oxford hospital for six weeks. The Chief Constable : All the more reason why we should find out why you assaulted the licensee in so cowardly a manner. The remand was granted.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
The Adjourned Hearing.

Prisoner was brought up again on Wednes- day morning, before Mr. J. W. Hayward, Alder- man Smethurst and Mr. C. Jackson Holmes.

He was further charged with having assaulted P.C. Coombs whilst in the execution of his duty. The Chief Constable outlined the case. He said that the house was very full and busy on the evening in question. Mr. Hankinson re- fused to serve prisoner, owing to his drunken condition and told him to leave the house. Pris- oner then began using filthy language, and the landlord at once went round the bar counter and was struck 114 prisoner in the mouth. While he was opening the door to put prisoner out,. the latter put his arm round his neck and bit him in-the lip, the teeth going,right through. Out in the street he struck Mr. Hankinson. again, and it ,was only- -the kindness of , a stranger that prevented him being even more seriously . hurt. P.C. Coombs came up. in re- Sponse to the landlord's . call' and .endeavoured. to take prisoner into custody; but-he had to get further help. Mr. Hankinson's shirt;: tie and vest were saturated with blood, 'and he bled till noon the following day. The assault was en-

tirely unprovoked. Mr. Hankinson bore out the Chief Constable's statement.

The prisoner interrupted with an indignant statement to the effect that Mr. Hankinson had thrown out an old lady with whom he (prisoner) and the old lady's husband had been drinking. He alleged that the landlord threw the old lady out on to the pavement, and that her head struck the .kerb and was injured. He alleged that the first violence between the landlord and himself came from the former, and contended that he did not bite Mr. Hankinson, but merely hit, him with his knuckle. Mr. Hankinson said that he had, earlier in the evening, ejected a woman who was using bad language to his (witness's) wife. P.C. Coombs gave evidence as to being called

to the spot and having a fierce struggle with prisonerovho kicked and bit him. He was mad drunk and was using most disgusting language. He had to be "frog-marched" to the Police Station, when help was obtained. Prisoner stated that he asked the policeman not to put the handcuffs on, as he was prepared to go quietly, but they took no notice of him. "What mercy did you have for a poor Irish- man?" the prisoner cried. "You don't like men in khaki, do you? I've been to the Front, and was at Landrecines: I was wounded and came out of Oxford Hospital." • The Chief Constable (to P.C. Coombs): Aren't there forty per cent. of your comrades in khaki, whatever he says about not liking men in khaki?—Witness: Yes. . .

And haven't the police the greatest respect for 'men in khaki?—Yes. Prisoner: I couldn't have been treated worse if I had been a German prisoner: You rushed into me like a dog and struggled till you got the handcuffs on. The police-sergeant who came to P.C. Coombs's assistance Said that the prisoner was "absolutely mad." Ile didn't suppose lie remem- bered a thing about the whole affair. He was carried bodily to the station. His clothes all came loose- with the violence of his struggles, and he was partially, naked when he. arrived at the station. Witness had never heard a man use viler language since he had been a police-

man. Prisoner, made a statement embodying the gist of the remarks he had made during the hearing of the evidence, but giving fuller details. The Court listened attentively to the loquacious Irishman for a matter of nearly ten minutes. and then, the Chief Constable having informed

" TERRIERS' " BANK FIGLIDAY.

Sports And Cricket On ThE East

Coast.

The 49th .Provisional Battalion (consisting of the Liverpool Scottish and the 4th and sth South Lancashires) had a very enjoyable sports meet- ing and cricket match, on Monday tmorning oit the Bast Coast. There was a large attendanco of the general public and some very close finishes were witnessed. Great amusement was caused by the potato, three-legged, ..and wheelbarrow races, and also the " bait the bear " corcipeti- tion. The cricket match was between teams representing the - South Lancashires and the Scottish, and the "Jocks" reversed last -week's result by coming out winners by 21 runs. Lance- Corporal. Jones bowled exceedingly well for tho winners,. taking 5 wickets at a cost of 8 runs. Scores : _ _

SERGEANTS AT CRICKET.

Another cricket match was played in the after- noon between picked teams of the Pevgeania. Perhaps it will be best not to divulge the scores as some of the county executive may be on the look-out for next season. It is sufficient to say that bruised shins were quite common, awl one very enthusiastic " etumper" tried to flop the ball with his nose—and succeeded.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

WARRINGTON'S FINAL DECISION.

At a meeting of the committee of tip+, War- rington r.e. on Wednesday evening it was unanimously decided not to take part in senior football for the duration of the war, the inten- tion of the committee being to encourage school- boys' and intermediate league competitions: - It is the intention of the Warrington Club Committee when the school holidays are over to confer with the committees of the Schoolboys' League and the Intermediate League to draw up a programme. This is the final reply' of the Warring-ter Club to suggestions coming from certain quarters that the northern Union League clubs should engage in friendly matches..

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 7, 1915.

'vYARRINGTOWS 111 C. HERO.

Telling Appeal To Young Men At Civic Presentation.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
TRIUMPHAL PROGRESS THROUGH THE TOWN.

Speeches At Liverpool And In Local Theatres.

"I only did my duty . . . and if occasion should arise I am quite- prepared to do it again!" Facing thousands of cheering admirers, Corporal Joseph Harcourt Tombs, Warrington's V.C., made this declaration and, with characteristic modesty, it was practi- cally the only. allusion that he did make to the deed of superlative gallantry which won for him the most prized of all military decora- tions—the little gun-metal cross "for valour!" The occasion was Saturday evening and the scene the front of the Town Hall, where the , huge crowd had assembled to witness the pre- sentation by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) to the brave Corporal of a beautifully executed and nicely framed illuminatel address, expressive of his fellow town.smen'e admiration of the deed of magnificent heroism which has won for him undying fame and decoration straight from the hands of his Bing. It was a memorable gathering—one that neither the central figure in it, nor any of the thousands who foregathered on the lawn front- ing the Town Hall entrance will ever forget, and of a verity the Corporal will take back with him to the trenches the echo of the deep- throated cheers which greeted him when he stood at the Mayor's side, facing the people— cheers that must almost have been as deafen- ing as the loud-voiced guns, 'midst whose boom-- ing he dashed out from the comparative safety of the cover of a fire-swept trench and, one by one, brought four of his wounded comrades out of deadly peril into a place where their dire hurts could be humanely attended- to.

meat of the Territorials, behind whom was an open carriage containing the gallant Corporal, the Mayor, the Deputy-Mayor, and the Town Clerk. The rear of the procession was brought up by a squad of members of the sth Super- numerary 2nd/4th South Lancashires, under the command of Lieut. Johnson.

The \procession, the •band playing suitable patriotic selections en route, wended its way through the west gate of the Park, and thence along Sankey-street and Buttermarket-street, as far as the Parish Church. It then returned to the Town Hall, via Mersey-street, Bridge-street, Itylands-street, Palmyra-smare, Parker-street, and Sankey-street. Cheering crowds lined the route, the press of people being specially dense in Church-street, where the hero is so particu- larly well-known, having made his home in Howley-lane, within a stone's throw of that thoroughfare.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
A DISTINGUISHED ASSEMBLAGE.

Yes, Corporal Tombs is a most modest hero; as one who has been privileged to talk to him on several recent occasions put it "He hasn't an atom of ' swank ' about him, and he just hates being made a 'fuss' of, though, of course, he is very proud to think that his fellow townsmen have been delighted to honour him. He's modesty itself, and the last thing he likes to talk about is the one thing that. all his fellow townsmen—wherever you met 'em—were talking about last week." Over the Town Hall the flag on which is emblazoned the Borough Arms was floating, and draping the railings in front of the terrace from which the speeches were made was a large Union Jack. In the carriage way a detachment of Territorials stood lined-up. The hero of the hour drove up with Mr. A. It. Stewart, the representative of the British Aluminium Co. in Warrington, whose guest the V.C. had been during the day. Among those, in addition to the Mayor, who assembled on the ,pp of the steps to witness the ceremony of presentation were the Deputy Mayor (Dr. Joseph), Aldermen James Evans, John Evans, Smethurst, Bennett, Pierpoint and Tinnion, and Councillors' Archer, Barton, Dickenson, Gough, Jenkins, Lindley, Roberts, Wilkinson, Wilson; Colonel Brereton Fair- clough (commanding the 4th Battalion South Lnacashire (T.F.) Regiment, who had returned from -the Front for a few days' rest), Mrs. Fair- ()lough, Colonel Moggridge, Colonel Moccata, Lieutenant 'Williamson, Mrs. James Evans, Mrs. John Evans, Mrs. Harold Joseph, Mrs. and Miss Tinnion, Mrs. Joseph Harrison, Miss Atkinson, Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk) and Miss Whittle, Mr. and Mrs. T. H. Spinks, Mr. and Mrs. Mathias, Mr. James Houghton, Mr. 11. Collin, Mr. Joseph Barton (Binning,- ham), Mr. John Holden and Mr. George Wallington. The Mayoress, who had hoped to accompany the Mayor, the Deputy Mayor and Corporal Tombs in the procession through the town, was unable to be present, as her, car broke down while on her return from Fair- bourne, but she reached Warrington in time to listen to the speeches made at the Twin Hall before the proceedings concluded.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS WORSHIP'S "PROUD PRIVILEGE."

The Mayor said: It is my proud privilege and duty, in the name of Warrington, to ask our gallant hero, Corporal Tombs, to accept as a slight recognition, this illuminated address, as a tangible proof that Warrington delights to honour its citizens who do gallant deeds. (Cheers.) The Victoria Cross is a much-coveted honour—an honour which very few obtain and an honour which very few of our gallant heroes live to receive. In the name of our town, Cor- poral Tombs, I rejoice that you are here, healthy, safe and sound, after your act of gal- lantry, and we hope, as we know will be the case, that many, many- more will try and emu- late your splendid example and win the Cross. (Cheers.) His Worship then shook hands heartily with the gallant Corporal. THE CORPORAL'S THANKS.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
"Only Did My Duty Out Of Spirit Of

Comradeship."

Advancing to the railing at the top of the stone steps and standing at attention, Corporal Tombs,' in a voice that carried across the wide carriage way and to the dense crowd on the greensward beyond, said: "Mr. Mayor, officers of the South Lancashire Regiment, and citizens of Warrington, I just wish to say how much I thank You and all the people of the town for the great honour you have done me. I also wish to thank you all for your great kindness and appreciation, which is shown by the num- bers in which you have turned up. I only have a few words to say: I only did my duty, and did it out of a spirit of comradeship, as I am pre- pared to do it again. (Cheers.) "Quite Prepared to do It Again!"

• "Yes, if occasion should arise, I am quite pre- pared to do it again. Lancashire lads have 'done themselves proud,' and everybody out in France speaks highly of them. Not only do they speak highly of my own regiment, but also of the South Laneashires, of whom we all in Warring- ton are specially proud. Both regiments have done noble work, and I think they deserve all the praise they have had, and I am sure that the men of my regiment asd of the South Lanea- shires mean to stick to the good reputation we have won.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
A Word to the "Slackers,"

"I should just like to say this: That there are still so many of our young men in 'civilians' who ought to be going soldiering and fighting for their country I see them round here to- night—quite fit and able to go and fight. We need them out yonder in Flanders; we n..ed them in the Dardanelles. I am sure that later on if they don't go of their own free will they will be compelled to go. We don't want that compulsion. England has been a free country for years; indeed, England has a name as a free country the wide world over. We sing 'Britons never: never shall' be slaves,' but I act afraid that a good many ot the young men will find that they will have to be conscripts if they don't come forward voluntarily. We don't want to copy the French Army's example—we are free men, and we are fighting for freedom, our lives and our homes.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
It's Up to You Young Men!

"It's.up to you—you young men—te do your utmost; to come and help us to crush the laiser. He will need a lot of crushing yet! To those who are still left behind I want to say, 'We want you to come forward!' I only did my bit

The Mayor then tailed for three cheers for "our hero," which were lustily given. Corporal Tombs then lifted his rifle above his head with both hands and said, "Now, then, you chaps, come and learn to use this. If you don't know how I'll show you the way, and plenty of others besides me will also give You a lesson, or as many lessons as you want." (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150807_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
DRIVE THROUGH THE STREETS.

Three ringing cheers were then given, for the idol of the hour, after which a short proces- sion was fornied and the main streets were tra- versed. First came the R.A.0.8. Band, under the conductorship of Mr. Robert Smith. On the heels of the musicians followed a detach-

===== 0002601_19150807_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPORAL'S ROUSING APPEAL.

"Come Along; Shoulder The Rifle

And Make No Excuses."

A tremendous and, if possible, more enthusi- astic crowd awaited the return of the proces- sion to the ToWn Hall and listened to, and cheered to the echo, 'another pretty and well- delivered address by the V.C. hero, as well as to brief, but pOinted orations by the Mayor, the Deputy Mayor and 'Alderman Tinnion, Standing by the side of the gallant Corporal, who, as before, had his rifle—" a tried, trusted' and true friend," as .he himself said—with him, his Worship said the people of Warring- ton had assembled in their thousands to honour one of Warrington's heroes. He wanted them to show their appreciation of the Cor- poral's magnificent services to the country by doing all they possibly could to induce many of the young men who had not already done so, to join the Army.

"Fought For His Country And For You!" Surely, young men—those among you who are fit and strong--you ought to take your places alongside Corporal Tombs—our hero— and his gallant comrades-in-arms!" added his Worship. "Corporal Tombs fought for his country and for you. Moreover, he risked his life several times to save his comrades. That of itself—think what a glorious example it is! .—should be sufficient to induce many of you to join and serve the country. I say emphati- cally. that it is the duty of every young man who is eligible to join one of the Services, either the Army or the Nary, or to assist in turning out munitions. Let me urge upon you to show your appreciation of what 'Corporal Tombs has done by joining the Colours, and he will be perfectly satisfied." (Cheers.)

COME WILLINGLY-OR BE FETCHED!

Corporal Tombs then again addressed the throng, coining in for. another rousing recep- tion; indeed it is doubtful whether a louder cheer was ever heard in the old park. The Corporal began by introducing a wounded Australian soldier, who was seated next to him, and reminded the audience that he, himself, was an Antipodean: "I like to meet a fellow countryman and a comrade," the Corporal pro- ceeded, "and it was just the spirit of comrade- ship led me to go from our firing line to the assistance of my wounded comrades. I should, like all the young fellows present—un- less they are engaged in turning out munitions or in other Government' work—to do their duty to their country—and they know what that was; it is to come along, shoulder the rifle and make no excuses. Have you thought, of it this way? How will you feel, in years to come, when your children say to you, What did you do, daddy, in the war?' If you did nothing you.' will hang your head and he ashamed of yourself. You must either come along will- ingly or, later On, you will be fetched. We don't want that old press-gang system brought into force again. I can assure you that every man is needed in the firing line. I am quite ready to go back, but before I do se I want to hear from the people of Warrington not that ones and twos, but that hundreds have enlisted. (Cheers.) It ,is up to you to save your country. If you don't do your share I pity you. You cannot realise what we have had to go through in defence of your homes—our homes —and those you love best. Will You See the Country " Done In" By

taken, and the result will be known by the end of next month.

Volunteer or Pressed Man.

Every man ot military age will be visited, and he will have to give a reason why he is not on service. I would sooner be a volunteer than a' pressed man. Your country needs you, your Empire needs you, the civilised world needs you. (Cheers.) This is the greatest struggle we have ever had, but do we fully realise the importance of it? If we do not realise it soon it will be too late. I make an appeal to every man who is able to come forward at once and do his share, or else, as Corporal Tombs said just now, you will be asked by-and-by, not by your friends, but by your children—and that will be the most cutting question of all—" Daddy, what did you do in the war?" and you will have to answer ".Nothing." (Cheers.) The proceedings concluded with the singing of "God save the King."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans?

"Will you hang behind and see your country done in' by Germans? lam sure you will not! If you don't come forward it will he a hard struggle. It is hard enough already, but it will be harder still if you hang back. Lord Derby went to France only a few weeks ago, and he complimented the King's Liverpool men upon their splendid work, and said that Lancashire had done first class. Won't you come along? Just think what the country is doing for .you I don't wish to sound my own praises, but if you want, to know how I .won my much-coveted distinction I will tell you. It was in recogni- tion of what is called bravery and gallantry under shell fire. It was nothing but my duty. That is all I did. I ask you to read the staff officer's story which appears in the local papers. It will tell you everything I did. It gives a good description of the incident, and I have nothing to add to it, except to say that I did my duty. I wish you to come along and do yours, too—for King, country and the Empire to which you belong, and for the sake of thos,e you love at home." (Loud and prolonged cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150807_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
IMITATE AS WELL AS CHEER!

The Deputy-Mayor said that there were many among the audience who had cheered Corporal Tombs who could do something better and more useful than merely cheering, and that was to imitate him—imitate him, at any rate, to the extent of offering themselves to King and coun- try for service. There was an old proverb which said that "Imitation is the sincerest form of flattery." He did not mean to say that all the audience could imitate Corporal Tombs in doing what he did to obtain the coveted Victoria Cross, but hp would tell them what they could do. Every man and women present that even- ing who had health and strength could do his or her duty to the Empire in this most critical time; in these dark days, probably the darkest days that could possibly arise in the history of any country. If they did not rise to the occa- sion it was quite possible they would be com- pelled to, do so. He wanted to urge them to give their services to their country, so that England and the Whole of. the civilised world would no be in danger of being _subjected to one of the most accursed systems of government and thraldom that wan ever tried to be forced upon them. He hoped the young men present would pay heed to the appeal which had been so eloquently made by Warrington's V.C. (Cheers.) MEN WANTED MORE THAN EVER!

Alderman Tina ion made an earnest appeal to the young men within sound of his voice to lose no, time in going to the Police Station and en- listing. Men were wanted now more than at any time during the past twelve months. The terrible set-back Russia had experienced meant more to us and more to our children and women„ than probably many thought. Whatever we had wanted in men and munitions and money before, we wanted much more to-day. Tie de- clared solemnly and sincerely that every man who was of military age, who was not either serving or assisting in supplying munitions, ought to be ashamed of himself. In the matter of recruiting the women could do a lot, and it was the duty Of everyone of the fair sex to in- sist upon every eligible man joining the colours, because it was the women who needed protec- tion. We have a glorious, grand, noble and .splendid Fleet, said the Alderman, by way of conclusion, but with the wonderful ' ingenuity and organisation of the German nation it is not impossible that this country may be invaded, although it is not 'probable. What would that mean to our women? What they have to do is to insist upon every man who ought to join the colour i doing so. I will give those who have not yet come forvra,rci a hint. A census is being

TEXT OF TIIE ADDRESS.

The text of the address presented to the gal- lant Corporal is is follows:— County Borough of Warrington.

At a meeting of the Town Council held on the 26th day of July, 1915, the following reso- lution was unanimously passed, and a copy thereof was ordered to be presented to Cor- ixwal Tombs, V.C.: "The Mayor referred to the honour of the Victoria Cross having been conferred upon Lance-Corporal Joseph H. Tombs, of the Ist Battalion King's (Liverpool Regiment), of 17, Howley-lane, Warrington, for most conspicuous gallantry near Rue du Bois on May 16th, 1915. Resolved that the hearty congratulations of this. Council be ten- dered to Corporal Tombs, V.C., on the distin- guished honour conferred upon him, and an expression of the pride of Warrington in his great valour, and in the honour conferred upoit the town." Given under the common seal of the Cor- poration of Warrington, this 31st day of July 1915.

Perm PEACOCK. Mayor: JAMES LYON WEarn,E, Town Clerk.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
V.C. IN LIVERPOOL.

Manly Appeals To Young Men.

Warrington's V.C. Was the central figure in a recruiting campaign which was vigorously pur- sued in Liverpool on Tuesday. He was accorded a tremendous reception at each of the various open-air meetings which he addressed during the day and in the evening, and ,his manly ap- peal, delivered in simple yet direct language, made an impression which no doubt will fructify at thtt recruiting offices. He com- menced by adNessing a big crowd in Church- street shortly after noon, and both here and later at the Queen Victoria Monument and Cazneau-street his modest remarks were punc- tuated by btrsts of cheering from the throng. "I Only Did My Duty . . Do Yours!" "I have not come here to talk a lot about myself," he said, "I have just come to ask you te go and do the same. I only did my duty, and I want you to go and do yours. I went out and rescued my four wounded 'comrades purely out of a spirit of comradeship and a desire to do my duty. That i what I told my command- ing officer when he said he had .recommended me for the V.C. He said 'Haven't you got any- thing to say?' and I said, 'Well, sir, I thank you for what you have done, for me, but all I can say is that I only did my duty.' Although I am not a Liverpudlian, I am proud to belong to the King's Liverpool Regiment, which has the best name of any regiment out. in France. I am going out again to face the music, and I want you men to come and face it, too. It's not nice music, I can tell you, but it's music that has got to be faced, and it is far better to face it out there, than to have to face it over here, and that's what it will come to unless everyone does his duty." "Going Out to Face the Music Again !"

At the Church-street meeting he grasped two of the railings around St. Peter's churchyard as he spoke to a large crowd from an improvised platform, and in spite of cries of "Tell us how you won the V.C. !" he made only a brief and almost apologetic reference to personal affairs. "I have not 'come to say a lot about myself," he remarked. "I rescued four 'wounded com- rades under fire. The rmans had a machine gun trained on me; but I 'got back with the fourth man without a scratch. I simply did my duty, however, and I've now come out to ask you to do yours. I am going out soon to face the music again, and I want you men to come along and give us a hand. It's not nice music, but someone's got to face it, and better over yonder than here, where .the music will be—take my word for it—unless everyone does his part." _ .

" Jack's " Congratulations.

Among the many people who crowded around the V.C. following his little speech, for the pur- pose of shaking his hand was a strapping young sailor, who by vaulting briskly up, to the cor- poral's side supplied an. unreheacsed incident that greatly pleased the crowd. Both men doffed their caps, and the corporal, seizing upon the picturesque element of their meeting, turned to the spectators, and with his hand on the sailor's shoulder announced, "The two Services! We are comrades in arms and we're working to- gether to see this matter through." The ap- plause, was loud and prolonged. "Who' Is Going To Follow Me To France?" In the evening Corporal Tombs met with an- other rousing reception at the open-air meet- ing held at the junction of Cazneau-street and Scotland-road. But he also met with something better than cheers. "Who is going to follow me back to, France?" he called. "Who will come with me across the Channel and help to fight the Germans? Now, 'men, put up your hands," and at once eight or nine hands were raised, and keen-eyed and vigilant recruiting-sergeants and men made their way through the crowd to secure the would-be recruits "there and then." _

With a touch of pride, the V.C. spoke of the splendid name The King's (Liverpool Regiment) had earned in France. "It is known as the Iron Brigade,' but the boys are determined that it shall also be known as the Case-hardened Brigade ' ; they are going to see that The King's Liverpool shall have some more V.C.'s, and if you would be one of them then join now," 'urged the corporal. He went on: "I always watch the clock anxiously for the approach of eight o'clock, as that is about the time, when it is just going dark, that the Germans begin to test our strength and to pour out their ammunition. It has been said that the Germans are short of ammunition, but don't believe it. When they will pour, as I have seen them, about thirty rounds of shot at a single enemy aeroplane, they cannot be so shert.of ammunition,"

FOUGHT FOR PERU!

It is not generally known that Corporal Tombs, who joined the "Kings." in 1912, had his first taste of fighting some time before. Out of a spirit of pure adventure he offered his ser- vice whilst in South America to an army organ- ised by the Peruvians against a neighbouring State. "It was a squabble about a frontier line, or something," he lightly explained in the course of an interview. _ _

"It was a queer sort of army I was with, made up of all sorts and conditions of men. amongst them even a few Germans. The 'war' was very like many others that 'break out amongst those little South American States. They're great sport in their way, but when it comes to' real fighting the biggest ,battle I took part in there was like manceuvres compared with the fighting in France." In addition to the appeals made throughout the day by the Corporal, there were also speeches from Mr. F. L. Joseph and Messrs: Jamieson, Rltys, and Wynne Jones, and in the morning selections were given by Lord Derby's band, and in the evening by the band of the Bth Irish. The number of recruits enlisted during the day was well up to the average and considered not unsatisfactory.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY MEN ENLIST.

V.C. And Divine Aid For Those Who Do Their Duty.

." I tell you we are a powerful nation; but the Germans are just as clever as ns. I was told before /I went out to France that they couldn't shoct—that they were no marksmen. But don't you believe, it! They can! They don't stick at anything, and they are just as well equipped as

===== 0002601_19150807_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
we are. But they haven't the courage; they haven't the bulldog grip! And we want you to come along and show the same courage as our other soldiers, and smash them until there isn't another one left."

So spoke Warrington's V.C. at an open-air reoruiting meeting held at dinner-time on Wednesday near the offices of Messrs. Peter Walker and Son in Dallo.m-lane. A consider- able crowd of workmen gathered.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
MAKING UP LOST TIME.

Mr. J. E. Wilson (of that firm), who presided, alluded to the fact that that day was the anni- versary of the outbreak of war; and went on to say that it seemed obvious the war would continue for a considerable period. Of course, they knew it was a grave struggle, and many of our brave soldiers had been killed in all parts; and, of course, they also knew that we had been unprepared. And at the present time, although we were making provision as, fast as possible— together with all the other Allies—we had still a big task before us. However, we were doing our best, and he thought it very creditable to Warrington that it had sent such splendid forces, and that they had fought so well. Lance- Corporal Tombs had risked his life on many occasions to save others; and although all the Warrington men had not got the V.C., they had done'heroic work. Each man might do his "hit." Even if they were not fit for serving their country abroad they might still be ac- cepted for service at home. Had we not joined in the war the Germans would have been' in Paris to-day. Our Navy also had stopped the Germans from getting control of the seas and from capturing the food, fop. which we had to be thankful.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
Another Chance for Rejected.

Although the war had been going on for twelve months, the German Emperor was in prac- tically the same position as when he started. However, we were stronger, inasmuch as in the meantime we had been making provision, and it did not follow that should anybody decide to throw in his lot with the Army he would be called upon to fight. But everybody of service- able age, without any great considerations from family ties, ought to defend his country in some shape or form, either by foreign service or by joining the National Reserve.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-MORROW TOO LATE!

Corporal Tombs said he had come along at the request of Lord Derby. Recruits were most urgently needed. It was all very well to say, "Oh ! come to-morrow !" To-inorrow might be too late! On behalf of his comrades in the trenches ho asked them to come along and fill up the gaps. Would they like to see all their brave fellows lying dead because there had been no reinforcements? There were plenty present he knew personally, who were not working in any munition shop. Let them try and persuade their " boss" to let them go; many of the " bosses " were quite willing to help them to go when they wanted to. It was "up" to every young man—not only in Warrington, not only in Lancashire, but-in the whole British Em- pire—to help, as he had told the people of Liver- pool the previous day. That had been his proudest moment; he was speaking with a man in the blue uniform—one of his greatest friends in the Navy, whom he kne/ before ever he thought of soldiering, No "Eight Hours a Day" in Army.

The Navy had a greater task than the Army had. But the Army must always be ready. They might be called upon in the .middle of a meal. They never troubled about eight hours a day or " anything "; it might be twelve hours or eightben hours! The country considered what he had done a great deed, but he had only done his duty. One of the first things that a soldier had to learn was obedience and .to do his duty. That was what he enlisted for. He wanted them to Come along and show the same spirit, and save their countrymen—and perhaps their brothers. Would not they rather be fight- ing for those at home than standing at the street corners, perhaps without a job after- wards? Wouldn't they come and fight for right against might? He didn't stand there to preach like the Raiser. The King of kings would carry them through just as He had carried him through, and he (the speaker) would be proud to ,shake their hand when they enlisted. They were all brothers, and they had :just as much chance as he had—any day out there—of get- ting the same distinction as the King had con- ferred upon him. All had the same chance!

===== 0002601_19150807_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
Not " Pleased with Himself."

Many people had said to him: "You must be highly pleased with yourself, aren't you?" He was not at all highly pleased with himself; ..he was proud of his townsmen, that was all, and he thanked them very heartily for their real warm Lancashire welcome. He had felt it. But let them come and stand by their brothers. Let them join the South Lancashire Regiment— for it had a fine name—or some other regiment. In that famous retreat from Mons his regiment had the work of scotching the Germans; when the cavalry were shot down and the Uhlans came charging up, it was B Company of the Ist King's that had to do the ticklish work. And yet, as they got their machine guns into posi- tion and did it, plenty of others were asleep in their beds at home!

===== 0002601_19150807_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT THREE MONTHS WILL DECIDE THE WAR.

Captain Afathans, of the 4th South Lance- shires, said that unless they sent men from Warrington to fill the Reserve Battalion, the Warrington Battalions—and others—who were ready to fight, would never be able to do so because of the lack of reserves. There was not a man there who did not know that the next three months would be the critical months of the war; and if they failed then, then they failed for good in the war! "We want you!" concluded the Captain. •

CORPORAL TOMBS VISITS STAR

KINEMA.

The Y.C. hero on Tuesday night visited the Star -Kinema House, Church-street, and was accorded a bumper reception by a crowded audi- ence. In the ccuirse of a few remarks, which were enthusiastically applauded, he made a strong appeal to those among his hearers who were able to join the colours to do so, and be concluded his brief exhortation with the words "For god's sake, do come and enlist !"

===== 0002601_19150807_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
AT THE HIPPODROME.

At the Hippodrome on Wednesday night, second house, Corporal Tombs was introduced by Councillor Archer, who said that the pride, they in Warrington felt in the 4th South Lanca- shires, who had acted their part to a man, gave an added warmth to their admiration for one of the Empire's sons who had won the most coveted of all military honours. Before call- ing on Corporal Tombs to speak a brief message, Councillor Archer read a resolution supporting the Government in its policy for the continua- tion of the war. The resolution was carried.

Corporal Tombs, in a short and crisp speech, appealed for recruits. "I come," he said, "to make an appeal to you for my comrades at the Front. I ask you, all of you, who are eligible for i service in the force and who are not working n a munition shop, to come along. You can't imagine the bloody struggles of the campaign: you can't realise what the boys have had, and are having, to suffer out there. There are breaches in the ranks that must be filled. T ask you, on behalf of my comrades and of your loved ones at home, please come !" (Applause.) "God same the King" concluded the ceremony.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

A GREAT " " AT THE COURT.

"A Chinese Honeymoon" has become a kind of classic amongst musical comedies. Certainly it has scored the biggest success ever registered by a play of its class, and it has now held "the boards" without, we believe, a single interim, since the days when Queen Victoria sat on the throne; indeed it had passed its zeilith when the sad news that she had passed away WAS flashed across the wires from Osborne Reuse just as a huge crowd was gathered outside the Strand Theatre waiting to get in to see the in- imitable Louie Freear as the equally inimitable Fi-Fi. To many local playgoers "A Chinese Honeymoon"' comes as an old and valued acquaintance, and though they may regret that the exigencies of the times—the two-show-a. night drama was not invented in the Victorian era—have necessitated 'curtailment of' the exces- sively quaint and adventure-full honeymoon, they cannot but rejoice that in the two-hour show whioh is being given nightly at the Royal Court this week, all the brightest of the songs. all the most piquant or the situatiops, and all the essence of the fun are preserved. Renewing one's acquaintance with as old friend, one cannot but re-opine the opinion he formed years ago, viz., that "A Chinese Honey- moon" was one of the most tuneful and merry musical irresponsibilities ever staged—a. verit- able feast of mirth and melody—and that it richly deserved the ,phenomenal success with which it met

The piece is this week being played to crowded. houses by a most capable company; indeed, everything about it—singing, dancing, acting, scenery, and costumiery—is excellent, and as Fi-Fi—the .one outstanding character— Miss Rachael Lowe is a tremendous success.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
Revue Next Week.

Revue is all the craze nowadays, and we are to have another specimen of the latest music- drama cult next week, in the shape of "Every- jog in the - Garden's !" We are promised a company of twenty-five artistes and a London beauty-chorus, to say nothing of a number of attractive specialities, and, judging from all that has been written about the piece, patrous of the popular local playhouse, to whom this particular kind of fare appeals, are in far a real treat.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0248.txt | included=true | included =====
SPLENDID ORGAN MUSIC AT THE

'DROME.

The 'outstanding item in the excellent pro- gramme, arranged by Manager Jackson at the Hippodrome this week, is supplied by Max Erard and Miss Zona Vevey. Miss Vevey sings attractive ballads and gives a monologue, while Max extracts altogether delightful melody from a splendidly-toned organ of big compass and abnormal size, and in the extractions displays the skill of the .thorough master. of the most soulful of all instruments. The organ has four full manuals and its cost is estimated at £2,000, and the volume of sound of which it is capable is a revelation as to tho possibilities of the power-blown wind-wood instrument when in capable hands. This turn proves deservedly and immensely popular, and one comes away from the theatre regretting that. exigencies of time did not permit of more of it, for both the organist and his charming collaborator are ex- cellent artistes, though im quite different Etpheres. Joe and Willy supply a brilliant comedy And balancing act, and Wylie, "the tramp musicfan," 'proves to be a consummate performer on a variety of instruments. Arthur Carnworth plays the banjo brilliantly, and Launcelot Quinn makes the most of a rick bari- tone voice. Altogether the bill this week is a fine one.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
" All-Star " Programme Next Week.

Charles Mott, the celebrated baritone, who has had the honour of singing before the King on many. occasions, will top an "all-star" bill at the Hippodrome next week. Another excel- lent turn, among the many excellent ones, will be that provided by Vivian Foster, who recently created something of/a furore at the' London Hippodrome, and who will appear in his big hit "Business As Usual." The Four Royal Scots, in the novel musical act, will form anOther feature, and The Mandos, in their "marvellous and sensational whirlwind act" also promise well. Several other artistes of repute will go to the making up of a first-class bill—one that should not be missed.

===== 0002601_19150807_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
HOLIDAY- PROGRAMMES AT THE STAR.

Star Kinema patrons have this week had sub- mitted to them two very fine programmes. A military drama entitled "Brother Officers" formed the big attraction for the early part of the week, while "The Club of Pharos" proved a most amusing 'comedy, and an excellent foil to the more serious fare. Other good pictures wero "In Lovely Algeria," "The Avenging Dentist," "The High Spots of Broadway," and "Pathe's Animated Gazette," which this week illustrates several incidents in the war..

The pictures for the latter half of the week— which will be shown at to-day's performances for the last time—include" The Middleman," a fine dramatic subject, featuring Albert Chevalier. The inimitable Charlie Chaplin appears in "Charlie, the Tramp," and a topical film shows the "Allied Fleets in the Dardanelles."

HIPPODROME PATRONS' PATRIOTIC DECLARATION.

Wednesday" being the anniversary of the declaration of war against Germany, the follow- ing resolution was passed at each performance at the Hippodrome:—" That on this anniver- sary of the declaration of a righteous war, this audience of the citizens in Warrington records its inflexible determination to continue to a victorious end the struggle in maintenance of those ideals of liberty and justice, which are the common and sacred cause of the Allies."

===== 0002601_19150807_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
Local Leather Trade.

Writing in the current issue the Warrington correspondent of the "-Leathlr Trades' Review" says: -The approach of the August holiday seemed to have small effect upon trade, the pressure being unrelieved up to the end of the week, and with a break strictly Confined to one day tanners were hard at it again yesterday trying to overtake the arrears of work. The demand for all Army leathers has just lately shown itself even more insistent than usual, and this not so much for deliveries for im- mediate use as for supplies right up to Novem- ber. The arrangement now established regulat- ing the fluctuation in price should make this distant business comparatively easy to work; unfortunately, however, it can do 'nothing to increase the supply, and this most vital part of the business is none too bright, it still being extremely difficult to get hold of the proper clasS of hide in any quantity. Present calls are all for quick deliveries, with numerous inquiries outside of contracts for odd supple- mentary lots, everyone being apparently kept a little short of full requirements, and dis- tributors are having a lively time hunting up these oddments. 'General trade is very good, with demand running remarkably even all round; middle-weight bends have been seiner; easily up to full capacity, and iiqht grades, which are largely going for equipm.mit woik, were in keen request, with offers poth seL..ree •and small. The home trade eontinus to in- quire for lower-priced stuff, including buffaloes, and there seems to be a good opening for this class of leather; but the unfortunate part is that tanners have very small oppor- tunity now to worklin anything which will not serve military requirements. A public. auction of hides imported from Prance under Govern- ment ,auspices is announced to take place in Liverpool shortly, giving a welcome supplement to our supplies of heavy pelts."

MONEY TAT 17.413, 1 ill Whilst it is clearly the duty of the Government to bring home A to the community the facts concerning the value of money during the present crisis, it is equally important that the community should take notice of the Government's instruetions. qThe splendid response to the War Loan is evidence diof the people's desire to help. There are thousands, however, who are making more wages at the present time than they ever made before, or are likely to make in the future, and who have not yet realized the necessity for saving. If each worker will set aside his extra earnings and deposit them in the Savings Bank, he will not only be helping to provide " silver bullets" to beat the Germans, but he will be making it easier for himself, his wife and children to overcome the slump in trade when the war is over. 1 - (I If you have a Savings Bank Account, and know that you can withdraw a little money when ills needed, it will make your life happier and give you an independence which a person in debt never has, or can ever hope to have. It is quite true that money "talks." ' A. Fr YOUR. SIMEIVICE: The Warrington, Runcorn & District SAVINGS . „.. ESTABIASHED 1818. Head Office . BUTTERMARKET-STREET. The Health Committee may yield to reason someday. HARDY PERENNIALS. For the next meeting of the Council, on Tuesday, two notices of motion appear on the agenda—one , Councillor Gough's famous motion to rescind a certain resolution, respect- ing the charges for water to manufacturers, and the other by Councillor Pemberton re- garding the terms of admission to the baths. We might be spared these motions, if not for the duration of the war, at any rate for the holiday month of August. TRAM PROFITS. The net profits in the Tramways Depart- ment for the year 1914-15 are £2,499, against £3,229 last yearl owing to decline in receipts. In the circumstances, says the Borough Treasurer, the decline in the receipts is not excessive; the effect of the temporary loss of population is shown upon all routes with the exception of Latchford, where the receipts have increased nearly one-halfpenny per car mile.

WORKS ASSOCIATION. FOURTH ROUND OF " FINLAYSON" CUP COMPETITION. The fourth round of the " Finlayson" Cup Competition in connection with the Warring- ton Works' Amateur Bowling Association, was completed on Saturday, on the Horse and Jockey ground, Winwick-road. Four interest- ing games were played. The Assurance Agents No. 1 combination played well and care- fully, and succeeded in knocking out the Cheshire Lines second string by the narrow margin of five points. The Whitecross Netting men put up a game struggle against Cros- field's (Cheshire)—who have been going strong throughout the competition—but had to I acknowledge defeat by a dozen points. Monks, Hall and Fletcher, Russell are old rivals and• this time the honours fell to Monks, Hall by the substantial margin of 35 points. The fourth and last game of the afternoon was between Pearson and Knowles' (Bewsey Forge) team and Crosfield's Soap Department, when the soapmakers surprised their supporters by being beaten by 32 points. Saturday August 28th, will see a start made with the merit competition in connection with the association. The principal award in this competition Consists of the 'Major Crosfield" Shield. A second War Fund handicap is being arranged for early in next month and it is to be hoped that it will prove as successful as did ,he one recently held.

A NIATEUR ASSOCIATION. Conservatives Successful In Club Championship Semi-final. On the Conservative Club green in Sankey- street on Saturday afternoon the Senior Con- servatives met the Bull's Head combination in the semi-final of the Club Championship con- test in connection with the Warrington and District Amateur Bowling Association. The Conservatives, like their green, were in fine form, and ran out winners by the big margin of 86 points, thus exacting ample revenge for their previous defeat at the hands of the Bull's Head players, and winning on the home and home games by 31. IN THE COURTS.—Continued. them that the prisoner had been discharged from the Army with an ." indifferent" character —which meant very bad—was informed by the Chairman that he would be fined Vs. Gd. or go to prison for 14 days. The Bench thought, added the Chairman, that it was a disgrace to Warrington that a man should be found "mad" drunk. 'Prisoner himself was chiefly to blame. MOTORISTS' OFFENCES. At the Warrington Police Court yesterday, Joseph Williamson, of 53, Fairbank-road, Brad- ford, was fined 20s. for driving a motor-cycle at a dangerous speed, in Knutsford-road, on July 24th. P.C. Skinner stated that defendant's speed was between 20 and 25 miles an hour.— Oswald John Clarke, of 605, Liverpool-road, Irlam, who did not appear to his summons, was , fined 40s. for driving a -motor car without a light in Wilderspool-road, on July 9th.
